The **Iraq War** ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): حرب العراق, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic): *ḥarb al-ʿirāq*), also referred to as the **Second Gulf War** (**Gulf War II**),[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92] was a protracted armed conflict in [Iraq](7515928.md) from 2003 to 2011. It began with [the invasion](201936.md) by a [United States–led coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-National_Force_%E2%80%93_Iraq), which resulted in the overthrow of the [Ba'athist government](26215470.md) of [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein). During the US [occupation of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Iraq_\(2003%E2%80%932011\)), the conflict persisted [as an insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_insurgency_\(2003%E2%80%932011\)) that arose against coalition forces and the newly established Iraqi government. US forces [were officially withdrawn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_United_States_troops_from_Iraq_\(2007%E2%80%932011\)) in 2011. In 2014, the US became re-engaged in Iraq, leading a new coalition under [Combined Joint Task Force – Operation Inherent Resolve](44618350.md), as the conflict evolved into the ongoing [Islamic State insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_insurgency_in_Iraq_\(2017%E2%80%93present\)).

The Iraq invasion was part of the [Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_W._Bush)'s broader [war on terror](13425800.md), launched in response to the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md). In October 2002, the US Congress passed [a bipartisan resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authorization_for_Use_of_Military_Force_Against_Iraq_Resolution_of_2002) granting Bush authority to use military force against Iraq. The war began on March 20, 2003, when the US, [joined by the UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_involvement_in_the_Iraq_War), [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_involvement_in_the_Iraq_War), and [Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_involvement_in_the_Iraq_War), initiated a "[shock and awe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_and_awe)" bombing campaign. Coalition forces launched a ground invasion, defeating Iraqi forces and toppling the Ba'athist regime. Saddam Hussein was [captured in 2003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_Saddam_Hussein) and [executed in 2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Execution_of_Saddam_Hussein).

The fall of Saddam's regime created a power vacuum, which, along with the [Coalition Provisional Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Provisional_Authority)'s mismanagement, fueled a [sectarian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_civil_war_\(2006%E2%80%932008\)) between Iraq's [Shia](22350732.md) majority and [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) minority, and contributed to a lengthy insurgency. In response, the US deployed an additional 170,000 troops during the [2007 troop surge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_troop_surge_of_2007), which helped stabilize parts of the country. In 2008, Bush agreed to withdraw US combat troops, a process completed in 2011 under President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama).

Among the [international law](8195726.md) justifications advanced by the United States for the Iraq War were the inherent right of [anticipatory self-defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anticipatory_self-defense) and prior authorization by the [Security Council](31956.md). The primary [rationale for the invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationale_for_the_Iraq_War) centered around false claims that [Iraq possessed weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction) (WMDs) and that [Saddam Hussein was supporting al-Qaeda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein_and_al-Qaeda_link_allegations). The [9/11 Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9/11_Commission) concluded in 2004 that there was no credible evidence linking Saddam to al-Qaeda, and no WMD stockpiles were found in Iraq. These false claims [faced widespread criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_the_Iraq_War), in the US and abroad. [Kofi Annan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kofi_Annan), then secretary-general of the [United Nations](31769.md), declared the invasion illegal under international law, as it violated the [UN Charter](88805.md). The 2016 [Chilcot Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chilcot_Report), a British inquiry, concluded the war was unnecessary, as peaceful alternatives had not been fully explored. Iraq held [multi-party elections in 2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2005_Iraqi_parliamentary_election), and [Nouri al-Maliki](4723094.md) became Prime Minister in 2006, a position he held until 2014. His government's policies alienated Iraq's Sunni minority, exacerbating [sectarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sectarian) tensions.

The war led to an estimated [150,000 to over a million deaths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casualties_of_the_Iraq_War), including over 100,000 civilians, with most occurring during the post-invasion insurgency and civil war. The war had lasting geopolitical effects, including the emergence of the extremist [Islamic State](9087364.md), whose rise led to the [2013–2017 War in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Iraq_\(2013%E2%80%932017\)). The war damaged American international reputation, and Bush's popularity declined. UK prime minister [Tony Blair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Blair)'s support for the war diminished his standing, contributing to his [resignation in 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Labour_Party_leadership_election_\(UK\)).

## Background

Main article: [Prelude to the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prelude_to_the_Iraq_War)

See also: [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War), [Iraq and weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction), [Iraqi no-fly zones conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_no-fly_zones_conflict), and [International sanctions against Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_against_Iraq)

Following the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War), the [United Nations](31769.md) passed 16 Security Council resolutions calling for the complete elimination of Iraqi weapons of mass destruction. Iraqi officials harassed inspectors and obstructed their work,[^cite_note-Zilinskas-93] and in August 1998, the Iraqi government suspended cooperation with the inspectors completely, alleging that the inspectors were spying for the US.[^cite_note-Fisk2007-94] The spying allegations were later substantiated.[^cite_note-95]

In October 1998, removing the Iraqi government became official [US foreign policy](7564.md) with the enactment of the [Iraq Liberation Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Liberation_Act). The act provided \$97 million for Iraqi "democratic opposition organizations" to "establish a program to support a transition to democracy in Iraq."[^cite_note-96] This legislation contrasted with the terms set out in [United Nations Security Council Resolution 687](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_687), which focused on weapons and weapons programs and made no mention of regime change.[^cite_note-97]

One month after the passage of the Iraq Liberation Act, the US and UK launched a bombardment campaign of Iraq called [Operation Desert Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Desert_Fox). The campaign's express rationale was to hamper Saddam Hussein's government's ability to produce chemical, biological, and nuclear weapons, but US intelligence personnel also hoped it would help weaken Saddam's grip on power.[^cite_note-98]

Following the [2000 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_presidential_election) of [George W. Bush](3414021.md), the US moved towards a more aggressive Iraq policy. The [Republican Party](32070.md)'s campaign platform in the 2000 election called for "full implementation" of the Iraq Liberation Act as "a starting point" in a plan to "remove" Saddam.[^cite_note-99] Little formal movement towards an invasion occurred until the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md), although plans were drafted and meetings were held from the first days of his administration.[^cite_note-Woodward2004Chap1-100][^cite_note-101]

### Pre-war events

Main articles: [Rationale for the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationale_for_the_Iraq_War), [Preparations for the 2003 invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preparations_for_the_2003_invasion_of_Iraq), and [Failed Iraqi peace initiatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Failed_Iraqi_peace_initiatives)



Excerpt from Donald Rumsfeld memo dated 27 November 2001

[^cite_note-tv.msnbc.com-102]

Following the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md), the [Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_W._Bush)'s national security team debated an Iraq invasion. On the day of the attacks, [US Secretary of Defense](43998.md) [Donald Rumsfeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Rumsfeld) asked his aides for "best info fast. Judge whether good enough hit [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) at the same time. Not only [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden)."[^cite_note-103] The next day, Bush ordered [White House](33057.md) counterterrorism coordinator [Richard A. Clarke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_A._Clarke) to investigate possible Iraqi involvement in the September 11 attacks, believing that a devastating attack like 9/11 involved a state sponsor.[^cite_note-104]

Bush spoke with Rumsfeld on 21 November and instructed him to conduct a confidential review of [OPLAN 1003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Central_Command_OPLAN_1003-98), the war plan for invading Iraq.[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-Schlosser_2023_6–25-106] Rumsfeld met with General [Tommy Franks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommy_Franks), the commander of [US Central Command](338482.md), on 27 November to go over the plans. A record of the meeting includes the question "How start?", listing multiple possible justifications for a US–Iraq War.[^cite_note-tv.msnbc.com-102][^cite_note-107] Bush began laying the public groundwork for an invasion of Iraq in his January 2002 [State of the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_the_Union) address, calling Iraq a member of the [Axis of Evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_of_Evil), and saying that the US "will not permit the world's most dangerous regimes to threaten us with the world's most destructive weapons."[^cite_note-108]

The intelligence community, however, indicated that there was no evidence linking Saddam Hussein to the September 11 attacks and there was little evidence that Iraq had any collaborative ties with Al Qaeda.[^cite_note-waas-109][^cite_note-National_Commission_on_Terrorist_Attacks_Upon_the_United_States_2004-110]<sup>: 334 </sup> Ultimately, the rationale for invading Iraq as a response to 9/11 has been widely refuted, as there was no cooperation between [Saddam Hussein and al-Qaeda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam%E2%80%93al-Qaeda_conspiracy_theory).[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112][^cite_note-Britannica-113][^cite_note-114][^cite_note-\:0-115][^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117]

A 5 September 2002 report from Major General Glen Shaffer revealed that the [Joint Chiefs of Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Chiefs_of_Staff)'s J2 Intelligence Directorate had concluded that the United States' knowledge on different aspects of Iraq's WMD program ranged from essentially zero to \~75%, and that knowledge was weak on aspects of a possible nuclear weapons program: "our assessments rely heavily on analytic assumptions and judgment rather than hard evidence."[^cite_note-IBTJ2memoStory-118][^cite_note-ShafferJ2memo-119] Similarly, the British government found no evidence that Iraq possessed nuclear weapons or any other weapons of mass destruction and that Iraq posed no threat to the West, a conclusion British diplomats shared with the US government.[^cite_note-120] The US intelligence community was of the opinion that Iraq had no nuclear weapons, and had no information about whether Iraq had biological weapons.[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122][^cite_note-nelson-123][^cite_note-124]

Vice President [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney) and Defense Secretary Rumsfeld expressed skepticism toward the [CIA](5183633.md)'s intelligence and accuracy to predict threats, and instead preferred outside analysis with intelligence supplied by the [Iraqi National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_National_Congress), which alleged that Saddam was pursuing WMD development and had ties to [al-Qaeda](1921.md).[^cite_note-\:5-125] Bush began formally making his case to the international community for an invasion of Iraq in his 12 September 2002 address to the [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council).[^cite_note-126]



A [United Nations](31769.md) weapons inspector in [Iraq](7515928.md) in 2002

In October 2002, the [US Congress](31756.md) passed the [Iraq Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Resolution).[^cite_note-127] Key US allies in [NATO](21133.md), such as the United Kingdom, agreed with the US actions, while [France](5843419.md) and [Germany](11867.md) were critical of plans to invade Iraq, arguing instead for continued diplomacy and weapons inspections. After considerable debate, the UN Security Council adopted a compromise resolution, [UN Security Council Resolution 1441](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council_Resolution_1441), which authorized the resumption of weapons inspections and promised "serious consequences" for non-compliance. Security Council members France and [Russia](25391.md) made clear that they did not consider these consequences to include the use of force to overthrow the Iraqi government.[^cite_note-128] The US and UK ambassadors to the UN publicly confirmed this reading of the resolution.[^cite_note-129]

Resolution 1441 set up inspections by the [United Nations Monitoring, Verification and Inspection Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Monitoring,_Verification_and_Inspection_Commission) (UNMOVIC) and the [International Atomic Energy Agency](14984.md). Saddam accepted the resolution on 13 November and inspectors returned to Iraq under the direction of UNMOVIC chairman [Hans Blix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Blix) and IAEA Director General [Mohamed ElBaradei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_ElBaradei). As of February 2003, the IAEA "found no evidence or plausible indication of the revival of a nuclear weapons program in Iraq"; the IAEA concluded that certain items which could have been used in nuclear enrichment centrifuges, such as aluminum tubes, were in fact intended for other uses.[^cite_note-130] In March 2003, Blix said progress had been made in inspections, and no evidence of WMD had been found.[^cite_note-blix1-131]



[US Secretary of State](32293.md) [Colin Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Powell) holding a model vial of [anthrax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthrax) while giving a presentation to the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md)

On 5 February 2003, [Secretary of State](32293.md) [Colin Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Powell) appeared before the UN to [present evidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_and_the_Iraq_War#Colin_Powell's_presentation) that Iraq was hiding unconventional weapons. Despite warnings from the [German](11867.md) [Federal Intelligence Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bundesnachrichtendienst) and the British [Secret Intelligence Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secret_Intelligence_Service) that the source was untrustworthy, Powell's presentation included information based on the claims of [an informant codenamed "Curveball"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curveball_\(informant\)), who later admitted that his claims had been false.[^cite_note-132] Besides claiming a relationship between al-Qaeda and Iraq, Powell also alleged that al-Qaeda was attempting to acquire weapons of mass destruction from Iraq.[^cite_note-133]

As a follow-up to Powell's presentation, the United States, the United Kingdom, [Poland](22936.md), [Italy](14532.md), [Australia](4689264.md), [Denmark](11746442.md), [Japan](15573.md), and [Spain](26667.md) proposed a resolution authorizing the use of force in Iraq, but NATO members like [Canada](5042916.md), France, and Germany, together with Russia, strongly urged continued diplomacy. Facing a losing vote as well as a likely veto from France and Russia, the US, the UK, Poland, Spain, Denmark, Italy, Japan, and Australia eventually withdrew their resolution.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135]

In March 2003, the United States, the UK, Poland, Australia, Spain, Denmark, and Italy began preparing for the invasion of Iraq with a host of public relations and military moves. In an address to the nation on 17 March 2003, Bush demanded that Saddam and his two sons, [Uday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uday_Hussein) and [Qusay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qusay_Hussein), surrender and leave Iraq, giving them a 48-hour deadline.[^cite_note-136]

The UK [House of Commons](13828.md) held a debate on going to war on 18 March 2003 where the [government motion was approved 412 to 149](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Parliamentary_approval_for_the_invasion_of_Iraq).[^cite_note-137] The vote was a key moment in the history of the [Blair government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premiership_of_Tony_Blair), as the number of government MPs who rebelled against the vote was the greatest since 1846. Three government ministers resigned in protest at the war, [John Denham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Denham_\(politician\)), [Lord Hunt of Kings Heath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Hunt_of_Kings_Heath), and the then [Leader of the House of Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Commons) [Robin Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Cook).

### Opposition to invasion

Further information: [Criticism of the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_the_Iraq_War), [Legitimacy of the 2003 invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legitimacy_of_the_2003_invasion_of_Iraq), [Legality of the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legality_of_the_Iraq_War), and [Protests against the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protests_against_the_Iraq_War)

In October 2002, former [US President](24113.md) [Bill Clinton](3356.md) warned about the possible dangers of pre-emptive military action against Iraq. Speaking in the UK at a [Labour Party](19279158.md) conference he said: "As a preemptive action today, however well-justified, may come back with unwelcome consequences in the future... I don't care how precise your bombs and your weapons are when you set them off, innocent people will die."[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139] Of 209 House [Democrats](5043544.md) in Congress, 126 voted against the [Authorization for Use of Military Force Against Iraq Resolution of 2002](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Resolution), although 29 of 50 Democrats in the Senate voted in favor. Only one [Republican](32070.md) Senator, [Lincoln Chafee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Chafee), voted against. The Senate's lone Independent, [Jim Jeffords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Jeffords), voted against. Retired US Marine, former Navy Secretary and future US senator [Jim Webb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Webb) wrote shortly before the vote, "Those who are pushing for a unilateral war in Iraq know full well that there is no exit strategy if we invade."[^cite_note-140] [Pope John Paul II](23805.md) also publicly condemned the military intervention, as well as saying directly to Bush privately: "Mr. President, you know my opinion about the war in Iraq... Every violence, against one or a million, is a blasphemy addressed to the image and likeness of God."[^cite_note-141]



Anti-war protest in London, September 2002. Organized by the British [Stop the War Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stop_the_War_Coalition), up to 400,000 took part in the protest.

[^cite_note-142]

On 20 January 2003, French Foreign Minister [Dominique de Villepin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominique_de_Villepin) declared "we believe that military intervention would be the worst solution".[^cite_note-143] Meanwhile, [anti-war groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_anti-war_organizations) across the world organized public protests. According to French academic [Dominique Reynié](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominique_Reyni%C3%A9), between 3 January and 12 April 2003, 36 million people globally took part in almost 3,000 protests against the war in Iraq, with [demonstrations on 15 February 2003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/15_February_2003_anti-war_protests) being the largest.[^cite_note-Walgrave2010-144] [Nelson Mandela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Mandela) voiced his opposition in late January, stating "All that (Mr. Bush) wants is [Iraqi oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_oil)," and questioning if Bush deliberately undermined the U.N. "because the secretary-general of the United Nations \[was] a black man".[^cite_note-145]

In February 2003, the US Army's top general, [Eric Shinseki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Shinseki), told the Senate Armed Services Committee that it would take "several hundred thousand soldiers" to secure Iraq.[^cite_note-146] Two days later, US Defense Secretary [Donald Rumsfeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Rumsfeld) said the post-war troop commitment would be less than the number of troops required to win the war. Deputy Defense Secretary [Paul Wolfowitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Wolfowitz) said Shinseki's estimate was "way off the mark," because other countries would take part in an occupying force.[^cite_note-147]

Germany's Foreign Secretary [Joschka Fischer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joschka_Fischer), although having been in favor of stationing German troops in [Afghanistan](737.md), advised [Federal Chancellor](20890626.md) [Schröder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerhard_Schr%C3%B6der) not to join the war in Iraq. Fischer famously confronted United States Defense Secretary Donald Rumsfeld at the 39th [Munich Security Conference](1464620.md) in 2003 on the secretary's purported evidence for Iraq's possession of [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons_of_mass_destruction): *"Excuse me, I am not convinced!"*[^cite_note-148] Fischer also cautioned the United States against assuming that democracy would easily take root post-invasion; "You're going to have to occupy Iraq for years and years, the idea that democracy will suddenly blossom is something that I can't share. … Are Americans ready for this?"[^cite_note-149]

In July 2003, former US ambassador [Joseph C. Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_C._Wilson) published an op-ed challenging the Bush administration's claim that Iraq sought uranium from Niger, a key justification for the war. In apparent retaliation, officials leaked the identity of Wilson's wife, [Valerie Plame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valerie_Plame), an undercover CIA officer, [exposing her covert status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plame_affair). The resulting investigation led to the conviction of [Lewis "Scooter" Libby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_%22Scooter%22_Libby), Vice President Cheney's chief of staff, for perjury and obstruction of justice, and his sentence was commuted by President Bush.[^cite_note-150]

There were serious [legal questions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legitimacy_of_the_2003_invasion_of_Iraq) surrounding the launching of the war against Iraq and the [Bush Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_Doctrine) of [preemptive war](384984.md) in general. On 16 September 2004, [Kofi Annan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kofi_Annan), the Secretary-General of the United Nations, said of the invasion "...was not in conformity with the [UN Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Charter). From our point of view, from the Charter point of view, it was illegal."[^cite_note-151]

## Course of the war

### 2003 - Invasion

See also: [List of coalition military operations of the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_coalition_military_operations_of_the_Iraq_War), [Iraq War order of battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_order_of_battle), and [Anbar campaign (2003–2011)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anbar_campaign_\(2003%E2%80%932011\))



Map of the invasion routes and major operations/battles of the Iraq War through 2007

Main articles: [2003 invasion of Iraq](201936.md), [2003 in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_in_Iraq), and [Timeline of the 2003 invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_2003_invasion_of_Iraq)

The first [CIA](5183633.md) team entered Iraq on 10 July 2002.[^cite_note-operation1-152] This team was composed of members of the CIA's [Special Activities Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Activities_Division) and was later joined by members of the US military's elite [Joint Special Operations Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Special_Operations_Command) (JSOC).[^cite_note-plan2004-153] Together, they prepared for an invasion by conventional forces. These efforts consisted of persuading the commanders of several Iraqi [military divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_division) to surrender rather than oppose the invasion, and identifying all the initial leadership targets during very high risk reconnaissance missions.[^cite_note-plan2004-153]

Most importantly, their efforts organized the [Kurdish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurd) [Peshmerga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshmerga) to become the northern front of the invasion. Together this force defeated [Ansar al-Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ansar_al-Islam_in_Kurdistan) in [Iraqi Kurdistan](679693.md) before the invasion and then defeated the [Iraqi army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_army) in the north.[^cite_note-plan2004-153][^cite_note-154] The battle against Ansar al-Islam, known as [Operation Viking Hammer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Viking_Hammer), led to the death of a substantial number of militants and the uncovering of a chemical weapons facility at Sargat.[^cite_note-operation1-152][^cite_note-155]



US Marines escort captured enemy prisoners to a holding area in the desert of Iraq on 21 March 2003

At 5\:34 am [Baghdad](4492.md) time on 20 March 2003[^cite_note-156] (9\:34 pm, 19 March EST) the surprise[^cite_note-surpr-157] military invasion of Iraq began. There was no declaration of war.[^cite_note-decl-158] The [2003 invasion of Iraq](201936.md) was led by [US Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Army) General [Tommy Franks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommy_Franks), under the code-name Operation Iraqi Freedom,[^cite_note-oil-159] the UK code-name [Operation Telic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Telic), and the Australian code-name [Operation Falconer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Falconer). Coalition forces also cooperated with Kurdish Peshmerga forces in the north. Approximately forty other governments, the "[Coalition of the Willing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_of_the_willing_\(Iraq_war\))", participated by providing troops, equipment, services, security, and special forces, with 248,000 soldiers from the United States, 45,000 British soldiers, 2,000 Australian soldiers and 194 Polish soldiers from [Special Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Forces) unit GROM sent to Kuwait for the invasion.[^cite_note-160] The invasion force was also supported by Iraqi [Kurdish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurd) [militia troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshmerga), estimated to number upwards of 70,000.[^cite_note-MajPeltier-161]

According to General Franks, there were eight objectives of the invasion:

> First, ending the regime of Saddam Hussein. Second, to identify, isolate, and eliminate Iraq's weapons of mass destruction. Third, to search for, to capture, and to drive out terrorists from that country. Fourth, to collect such intelligence as we can relate to terrorist networks. Fifth, to collect such intelligence as we can relate to the global network of illicit weapons of mass destruction. Sixth, to end sanctions and to immediately deliver humanitarian support to the displaced and to many needy Iraqi citizens. Seventh, to secure Iraq's [oil fields](2910801.md) and resources, which belong to the Iraqi people. And last, to help the Iraqi people create conditions for a transition to representative self-government.[^cite_note-162]

The invasion was a quick and decisive operation encountering major resistance, though not what the US, British and other forces expected. The Iraqi regime had prepared to fight both a conventional and irregular, [asymmetric warfare](46863.md) at the same time, conceding territory when faced with superior conventional forces, largely armored, but launching smaller-scale attacks in the rear using fighters dressed in civilian and paramilitary clothes.

Coalition troops launched air and [amphibious assaults](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphibious_assault) on the [al-Faw Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Faw_Peninsula) to secure the oil fields there and the important ports, supported by warships of the [Royal Navy](26061.md), [Polish Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Navy), and [Royal Australian Navy](26327.md). The [United States Marine Corps](17349325.md)' [15th Marine Expeditionary Unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/15th_Marine_Expeditionary_Unit), attached to [3 Commando Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3_Commando_Brigade) and the Polish Special Forces unit [GROM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GROM), attacked the port of [Umm Qasr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_Qasr), while the [British Army](4887.md)'s [16 Air Assault Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16_Air_Assault_Brigade) secured the oil fields in southern Iraq.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164]

The [US 3rd Infantry Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_3rd_Infantry_Division) moved westward and then northward through the western desert toward Baghdad, while the [1st Marine Expeditionary Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_Marine_Expeditionary_Force) moved more easterly along Highway 1 through the center of the country, and [1 (UK) Armoured Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1_\(UK\)_Armoured_Division) moved northward through the eastern marshland.[^cite_note-165] The American 1st Marine Division [fought through Nasiriyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Nasiriyah) in a battle to seize the major road junction.[^cite_note-166] The US 3rd Infantry Division defeated Iraqi forces entrenched in and around [Talil Airfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Air_Base).[^cite_note-167]

With the Nasiriyah and Talil Airfields secured in its rear, the 3rd Infantry Division supported by the [101st Airborne Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/101st_Airborne_Division) continued its attack north toward [Najaf](200512.md) and [Karbala](210957.md), but a severe sand storm slowed the coalition advance and there was a halt to consolidate and make sure the supply lines were secure.[^cite_note-168] When they started again [they secured the Karbala Gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Karbala_Gap_\(2003\)), a key approach to Baghdad, then secured the bridges over the [Euphrates River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphrates_River), and US Army forces poured through the gap on to Baghdad. In the middle of Iraq, the 1st Marine Division fought its way to the eastern side of Baghdad and prepared for the attack to seize the city.[^cite_note-169]

On 9 April, Baghdad fell, ending Saddam's 24‑year rule. US forces seized the deserted [Ba'ath Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Socialist_Ba'ath_Party_%E2%80%93_Iraq_Region) ministries and, according to some reports later disputed by the Marines on the ground, stage-managed[^cite_note-Los_Angeles_Times-170] the [tearing down of a huge iron statue of Saddam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firdos_Square_statue_destruction). Allegedly, though not seen in the photos or heard on the videos, shot with a [zoom lens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoom_lens), was the chant of the inflamed crowd for [Muqtada al-Sadr](351058.md), the radical Shiite cleric.[^cite_note-171] The abrupt fall of Baghdad was accompanied by gratitude toward the invaders, but also civil disorder, including the [looting](209811.md) of public and government buildings and increased crime.[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173]

According to [the Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pentagon), 250,000 short tons (230,000 t) (of 650,000 short tons (590,000 t) total) of ordnance was looted, providing a significant source of ammunition for the [Iraqi insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_insurgency_\(2003%E2%80%9311\)). The invasion phase concluded when [Tikrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tikrit), Saddam's home town, fell with little resistance to the [US Marines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Marines) of [Task Force Tripoli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Task_Force_Tripoli) on 15 April.

In the invasion phase of the war (19 March – 30 April), an estimated 9,200 Iraqi combatants were killed by coalition forces along with an estimated 3,750 non-combatants, i.e. civilians who did not take up arms.[^cite_note-174] Coalition forces reported the death in combat of 139 US military personnel[^cite_note-175] and 33 UK military personnel.[^cite_note-176]

### Post-invasion phase

Main article: [Occupation of Iraq (2003–2011)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Iraq_\(2003%E2%80%932011\))

Further information: [Iraqi insurgency (2003–2011)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_insurgency_\(2003%E2%80%932011\))

#### 2003: Beginnings of insurgency

Further information: [Ramadi under US military occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadi_under_US_military_occupation)



A [Marine Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_Marine_Division) [M1 Abrams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M1_Abrams) tank patrols Baghdad after its fall in 2003



Humvee struck by an improvised explosive device attack in Iraq on 29 September 2004. Staff Sgt. Michael F. Barrett, a military policeman in Marine Wing Support Squadron 373, was severely injured in the attack



Polish [GROM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GROM) forces in sea operations during the Iraq War



Marines from D Company, 3rd Light Armored Reconnaissance Battalion guard detainees prior to loading them into their vehicle

Widespread looting and low-level criminal activity gripped the country in April 2003. By that point it was clear that there were not enough US forces to control the breakdown of order and little plan to restore it.[^cite_note-timeline-177][^cite_note-Porter-178]

On 1 May 2003, President Bush visited the [aircraft carrier](2219.md) [USS *Abraham Lincoln*](199106.md) operating a few miles west of [San Diego, California](28504.md) and declared an end to major combat operations in Iraq. At sunset, he held his nationally televised ["Mission Accomplished" speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mission_Accomplished_speech), delivered before the sailors and [airmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airmen) on the [flight deck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_deck).[^cite_note-Schlosser_2023_6–25-106] Ambassador [Paul Bremer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Bremer) arrived in Iraq on May 12, 2003 and established the [Coalition Provisional Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Provisional_Authority). One of his first actions was to initiate the [de-Ba'athification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De-Ba'athification) process.[^cite_note-timeline-177]

Nevertheless, [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) remained at large, and significant pockets of resistance remained. After Bush's speech, coalition forces noticed a flurry of attacks on its troops began to gradually increase in various regions, such as the "[Sunni Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Triangle)".[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180] [Muqtada al-Sadr](351058.md), the leader of a large anti-American faction in Baghdad's Sadr City, issued a [fatwa](11259.md) allowing his followers to partake in the looting provided a portion of their takings were gifted to the [Sadrist Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadrist_Movement).[^cite_note-Porter-178]

##### Coalition Provisional Authority and the Iraq Survey Group

See also: [Iraqi Governing Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Governing_Council), [International Advisory and Monitoring Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Advisory_and_Monitoring_Board), [CPA Program Review Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Provisional_Authority_Program_Review_Board), [Development Fund for Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Development_Fund_for_Iraq), and [Investment in post-invasion Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Investment_in_post-invasion_Iraq)

Shortly after the invasion, the multinational coalition created the [Coalition Provisional Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Provisional_Authority) (CPA; [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): سلطة الائتلاف الموحدة), based in the [Green Zone](856542.md), as a [transitional government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transitional_government) of Iraq until the establishment of a democratic government. Citing [United Nations Security Council Resolution 1483](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1483) and the [laws of war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laws_of_war), the CPA vested itself with executive, [legislative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative), and [judicial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial) authority over the Iraqi government from the period of the CPA's inception on 21 April 2003 until its dissolution on 28 June 2004.



Occupation zones in [Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Provisional_Authority) as of September 2003

The CPA was originally headed by [Jay Garner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Garner), a former US military officer, but his appointment lasted only until 11 May 2003, when President Bush appointed [L. Paul Bremer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L._Paul_Bremer). On 16 May 2003, his first day on the job, Paul Bremer issued [Coalition Provisional Authority Order 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Provisional_Authority_Order_1) to exclude from the new Iraqi government and administration members of the Baathist party. This policy, known as [De-Ba'athification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De-Ba'athification), eventually led to the removal of 85,000 to 100,000 Iraqi people from their jobs,[^cite_note-181] including 40,000 school teachers who had joined the Baath Party simply to stay employed. US army general [Ricardo Sanchez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Sanchez) called the decision a "catastrophic failure".[^cite_note-182] Bremer served until the CPA's dissolution in June 2004.

In May 2003, the US Advisor to Iraq Ministry of Defense within the CPA, [Walter B. Slocombe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_B._Slocombe), advocated changing the pre-war Bush policy to employ the former Iraqi Army after hostilities on the ground ceased.[^cite_note-183] At the time, hundreds of thousands of former Iraqi soldiers who had not been paid for months were waiting for the CPA to hire them back to work to help secure and rebuild Iraq. Despite advice from US Military Staff working within the CPA, Bremer asked Bush for authority to change the US policy. Bush gave Bremer and Slocombe authority to change the pre-war policy. Slocombe then announced [Coalition Provisional Authority Order 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Provisional_Authority_Order_2). The order led to the unemployment and alienation of hundreds of thousands of former armed Iraqi soldiers, many of whom felt betrayed after following pre-war instructions given to them on leaflets dropped on their positions by coalition aircraft telling them to lay down their arms and return to their homes.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185] Many of these soldiers subsequently aligned themselves with various occupation resistance movements. In the week before the order to dissolve the Iraqi Army, no coalition forces were killed by hostile action in Iraq; the week after, five US soldiers were killed. Then, on 18 June 2003, coalition forces opened fire on former Iraqi soldiers protesting in Baghdad who were throwing rocks at coalition forces. The policy to disband the Iraqi Army was reversed by the CPA only days after it was implemented. But it was too late; the former Iraqi Army shifted their alliance from one that was ready and willing to work with the CPA to one of armed resistance against the CPA and the coalition forces.[^cite_note-186]

Bremer then turned his attention to economic restructuring, issuing key edicts between May and September 2003 to rapidly transform Iraq's state-planned system into a free-market economy. Specifically, [Order 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_reform_of_Iraq) suspended all tariffs and trade restrictions through the end of 2003, [Order 37](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_reform_of_Iraq) set a universal 15 percent flat tax, [Order 39](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_reform_of_Iraq) opened non-oil assets to full foreign ownership, privatized 200 state enterprises, and enabled untaxed profit repatriation, while [Order 40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_reform_of_Iraq) permitted foreign banks to buy up to 50 percent of domestic Iraqi banks.[^cite_note-187]

Another group created by the [multinational force in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multinational_force_in_Iraq) post-invasion was the 1,400-member international [Iraq Survey Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Survey_Group), who conducted a fact-finding mission to find Iraq's weapons of mass destruction (WMD) programs. In 2004, the ISG's [Duelfer Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duelfer_Report) stated that Iraq did not have a viable WMD program.[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189][^cite_note-GuardianReport2-190][^cite_note-TelegraphReport2-191][^cite_note-Sands-192][^cite_note-193]

##### Ramadan Offensive 2003

Coalition military forces launched several operations around the [Tigris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tigris) River peninsula and in the Sunni Triangle. A series of similar operations were launched throughout the summer in the Sunni Triangle. In late 2003, the intensity and pace of insurgent attacks increased. A surge in guerrilla attacks ushered in an insurgent effort that was termed the "[Ramadan Offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan_Offensive_\(2003\))".

Fall 2003 saw major attacks at the Jordanian Embassy and the [bombing of UN Headquarters in Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombing_of_UN_Headquarters_in_Baghdad) in which [Sérgio Vieira de Mello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A9rgio_Vieira_de_Mello) was killed.[^cite_note-timeline-177] The three governorates with the highest number of attacks were [Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baghdad_Governorate), [Al Anbar](467036.md), and [Saladin](200537.md). Those three governorates account for 35% of the population, but by December 2006 they were responsible for 73% of US military deaths and an even higher percentage of recent US military deaths (about 80%).[^cite_note-194]

To counter this offensive, coalition forces began to use air power and artillery again for the first time since the end of the invasion, by striking suspected ambush sites and mortar launching positions. Surveillance of major routes, patrols, and raids on suspected insurgents was stepped up. In addition, two villages, including Saddam's birthplace of al-Auja and the small town of [Abu Hishma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Hishma), were surrounded by barbed wire and monitored.

#### Capturing former government leaders

See also: [Supreme Iraqi Criminal Tribunal](766565.md) and [Trial of Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trial_of_Saddam_Hussein)



[Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) being pulled from his hideaway in [Operation Red Dawn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Red_Dawn) on 13 December 2003

In summer 2003, the multinational forces focused on [capturing the remaining leaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Most-wanted_Iraqi_playing_cards) of the former government. On 22 July, a US raid] [killed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Qusay_and_Uday_Hussein) Saddam's sons, [Uday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uday_Hussein) and [Qusay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qusay_Hussein), along with one of his grandsons. In all, over 300 top leaders of the former government were killed or captured, as well as numerous lesser functionaries and military personnel.

[Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) was captured on 13 December 2003, on a farm near [Tikrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tikrit) in [Operation Red Dawn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Red_Dawn).[^cite_note-195] The operation was conducted by the US [4th Infantry Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4th_Infantry_Division_\(United_States\)) and members of [Task Force 121](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Task_Force_121). Intelligence on Saddam's whereabouts came from his family members and former bodyguards.[^cite_note-196]

With the capture of Saddam and a drop in the number of insurgent attacks, some concluded that multinational forces were prevailing in the fight against the insurgency. The provisional government began training the new Iraqi security forces intended to police the country, and the US promised over \$20 billion in reconstruction money in the form of a credit against Iraq's future oil revenues. Oil revenue was also used for rebuilding schools and for work on the electrical and refining infrastructure.

Shortly after Saddam's capture, elements left out of the Coalition Provisional Authority began to agitate for elections and the formation of an [Iraqi Interim Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Interim_Government). Most prominent among these was the [Shia](26961.md) cleric [Grand Ayatollah Ali al-Sistani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Ayatollah_Ali_al-Sistani). The Coalition Provisional Authority opposed allowing democratic elections at this time.[^cite_note-197] The insurgents stepped up their activities. The two most turbulent centers were the area around [Fallujah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fallujah) and the poor [Shia](26961.md) sections of cities from [Baghdad](4492.md) ([Sadr City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadr_City)) to [Basra](57580.md) in the south.

##### Looting of artifacts from Iraqi museums

Main article: [Archaeological looting in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological_looting_in_Iraq)

Following the US invasion of Iraq in 2003, large numbers of antiquities including the [Gilgamesh Dream Tablet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epic_of_Gilgamesh) were stolen, both from museums, such as the [Iraq National Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_National_Museum), but also because of illegal excavations at archeological sites throughout the country. Many of them were smuggled into the United States through the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) (UAE) and [Israel](9282173.md), contrary to federal law. [Donald Rumsfeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Rumsfeld) rejected the claim that they were removed by US military personnel. In the 2020s, about 17,000 artifacts were returned to Iraq from the US and Middle Eastern countries. But according to an Iraqi archeology professor at the [University of Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Baghdad), the repatriation of these items was only a partial success; the Baghdad office of the [United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization (UNESCO)](21786641.md) continues to search for the loot worldwide. Many Iraqis blame the [United States](3434750.md) for the loss of so many pieces of their country's history.[^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199]

#### 2004: Insurgency expands



Areas of Responsibility in Iraq as at 30 April 2004

Main article: [2004 in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_in_Iraq)

See also: [2004 Iraq spring fighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Iraq_spring_fighting) and [Fallujah during the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fallujah_during_the_Iraq_War)

The start of 2004 was marked by a relative lull in violence. Violence increased during the [Iraq Spring Fighting of 2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Spring_Fighting_of_2004) with foreign fighters from around the Middle East as well as [Jama'at al-Tawhid wal-Jihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jama'at_al-Tawhid_wal-Jihad), an [al-Qaeda](1921.md)-linked group led by [Abu Musab al-Zarqawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Musab_al-Zarqawi), helping to drive the insurgency.[^cite_note-200] As the violence intensified in March, there was a change in targeting from the coalition forces towards the new Iraqi Security Forces, as hundreds of Iraqi civilians and police were killed over the next few months in a series of bombings. In the bloodiest day of the war since the start, hundreds of Shi'a were killed when five bombs exploded on March 2 during [Ashoura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashoura) celebrations.[^cite_note-timeline-177]

The most serious fighting of the war so far began on 31 March 2004, when [Iraqi insurgents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_insurgency_\(2003%E2%80%932011\)) in [Fallujah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fallujah) ambushed a [Blackwater USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackwater_USA) convoy led by four US [private military contractors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_military_contractor) who were providing security for food caterers [Eurest Support Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurest_Support_Services).[^cite_note-201] The four armed contractors, [Scott Helvenston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Helvenston), Jerko Zovko, Wesley Batalona, and Michael Teague, were killed with grenades and small arms fire. Their bodies were dragged from their vehicles by locals, beaten, burned and their mutilated corpses hung over a bridge crossing the [Euphrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphrates).[^cite_note-202] Photos of the event were released to [news agencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/News_agency) worldwide, causing indignation and [moral outrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_panic) in the United States, and prompting an unsuccessful "pacification" of the city: the [First Battle of Fallujah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Battle_of_Fallujah) in April 2004.

Followers of the Shi'a mullah [Muqtada al-Sadr](351058.md) known as the [Mahdi militia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahdi_militia) paraded through multiple cities. In April 2004, the Shi'a demonstrators launched attacks on coalition targets in an attempt to seize control from Iraqi security forces. Southern and central Iraq began to erupt in urban guerrilla combat as multinational forces attempted to keep control and prepared for a counteroffensive. Several coalition troops died in [Sadr City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadr_City) and [Najaf](200512.md). These clashes lasted until June 2004.[^cite_note-timeline-177]



[Coalition Provisional Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Provisional_Authority) director [L. Paul Bremer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L._Paul_Bremer) signs over sovereignty to the appointed [Iraqi Interim Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Interim_Government), 28 June 2004

In June 2004, the CPA formally transferred sovereignty to the Iraqi government, headed by interim Prime Minister [Ayad Allawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayad_Allawi).[^cite_note-timeline-177] Allawi opposed the hasty de-baathification that would destabilize the political structure of the Iraqi government.[^cite_note-Porter-178] His secular rule of law agenda was unsuccessful as "instritutionalized sectarianism" developed in the escalating conflict with Muqtada al-Sadr in Najaf and Sunni radicals in Fallujah.[^cite_note-203]

In one of the most significant single attacks of the war 49 newly trained Iraqi soldiers were executed by insurgents wearing police uniforms on 23 October 2004. Analysts note this supports the view that Iraqi police forces and Interior Ministry had been compromised by insurgents. Allawi blamed the attack on coalition forces.[^cite_note-timeline-177]

The offensive in Fallujah resumed in November 2004 in the bloodiest battle of the war: the [Second Battle of Fallujah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Battle_of_Fallujah), described by the US military as "the heaviest [urban combat](319027.md) (that they had been involved in) since the [Battle of Hue City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Hue_City) in Vietnam."[^cite_note-204] During the assault, US forces used [white phosphorus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_phosphorus_use_in_Iraq) as an incendiary weapon against insurgent personnel, attracting controversy. The 46‑day battle resulted in a victory for the coalition, with 95 US soldiers killed and approximately 1,350 insurgents. Fallujah was totally devastated during the fighting, though civilian casualties were low, as they had mostly fled before the battle.[^cite_note-205]



At [Abu Ghraib prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ghraib_prison), members of the [US Army](32087.md) and the [CIA](5183633.md) physically, sexually, and psychologically [tortured and abused prisoners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ghraib_torture_and_prisoner_abuse) in US custody. The image of *[The Hooded Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hooded_Man)* symbolized the scanda&#x6C;*.*

Another major event of that year was the revelation of widespread [prisoner abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prisoner_abuse) at [Abu Ghraib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ghraib_prison), which received international media attention in April 2004. First reports of the [Abu Ghraib prisoner abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ghraib_prisoner_abuse), as well as graphic pictures showing US military personnel taunting and abusing Iraqi prisoners, came to public attention from a *[60 Minutes II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60_Minutes_II)* news report and a [Seymour M. Hersh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seymour_M._Hersh) article in *[The New Yorker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Yorker)* (posted online on 30 April).[^cite_note-206] Military correspondent [Thomas Ricks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_E._Ricks_\(journalist\)) claimed that these revelations dealt a blow to the moral justifications for the occupation in the eyes of many people, especially Iraqis, and was a turning point in the war.[^cite_note-207]

2004 also marked the beginning of [Military Transition Teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Transition_Teams) in Iraq, which were teams of US military advisors assigned directly to New Iraqi Army units.

#### 2005: Elections and transitional government

Further information: [2005 in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_in_Iraq) and [Military transition team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_transition_team)



Convention center for Council of Representatives of Iraq

On 31 January, Iraqis [elected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2005_Iraqi_legislative_election) the [Iraqi Transitional Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Transitional_Government) to draft a permanent constitution. Although some violence and a widespread Sunni [boycott](18591996.md) marred the event, most of the eligible Kurd and Shia populace participated. On 4 February, [Paul Wolfowitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Wolfowitz) announced that 15,000 US troops whose tours of duty had been extended in order to provide election security would be pulled out of Iraq by the next month.[^cite_note-208] February to April were relatively peaceful compared to the carnage of November and January, with insurgent attacks averaging 30 a day from the prior average of 70.

The [Battle of Abu Ghraib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Abu_Ghraib) on 2 April 2005 was an attack on US forces at Abu Ghraib prison, which consisted of heavy mortar and rocket fire, under which an estimated 80–120 armed insurgents attacked with grenades, small arms, and two vehicle-borne improvised explosive devices (VBIED). The US force's munitions ran so low that orders to fix bayonets were given in preparation for hand-to-hand fighting. It was considered to be the largest coordinated assault on a US base since the Vietnam War.[^cite_note-209]

Hopes for a quick end to the insurgency and a withdrawal of US troops were dashed in May, Iraq's bloodiest month since the invasion. Suicide bombers, believed to be mainly disheartened Iraqi Sunni Arabs, Syrians and Saudis, tore through Iraq. Their targets were often Shia gatherings or civilian concentrations of Shias. As a result, over 700 Iraqi civilians died in that month, as well as 79 US soldiers.

Summer 2005 saw fighting around [Baghdad](4492.md) and at [Tall Afar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tall_Afar) in northwestern Iraq as US forces tried to seal off the Syrian border. This led to fighting in the autumn in the small towns of the [Euphrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphrates) valley between the capital and that border.[^cite_note-Ricks-2006-210]

A referendum was held on 15 October in which the new [Iraqi constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Iraq) was [ratified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Iraqi_constitutional_referendum). An [Iraqi National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_National_Assembly) was [elected in December](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/December_2005_Iraqi_legislative_election), with participation from the Sunnis as well as the Kurds and Shia.[^cite_note-Ricks-2006-210]

Insurgent attacks increased in 2005 with 34,131 recorded incidents, compared to a total 26,496 for the previous year.[^cite_note-211]

#### 2006: Civil war and permanent Iraqi government

Further information: [Iraqi civil war (2006–2008)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_civil_war_\(2006%E2%80%932008\)), [Sectarian violence in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sectarian_violence_in_Iraq), [Operation Together Forward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Together_Forward), and [Provincial Reconstruction Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provincial_Reconstruction_Team)



[US Marines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USMC) from [3rd Battalion 3rd Marines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3rd_Battalion_3rd_Marines) clear a house in [Al Anbar Governorate](467036.md).

The beginning of 2006 was marked by government creation talks, growing sectarian violence, and continuous anti-coalition attacks. Sectarian violence expanded to a new level of intensity following the [al-Askari Mosque bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Askari_Mosque_bombing_\(2006\)) in the Iraqi city of Samarra, on 22 February 2006. The explosion at the mosque, one of the holiest sites in Shi'a Islam, is believed to have been caused by a bomb planted by al-Qaeda.

Although no injuries occurred in the blast, the mosque was severely damaged and the bombing resulted in violence over the following days. Over 100 dead bodies with bullet holes were found on 23 February, and at least 165 people are thought to have been killed. In the aftermath of this attack, the US military calculated that the average homicide rate in [Baghdad](4492.md) tripled from 11 to 33 deaths per day. In 2006 the UN described the environment in Iraq as a "civil war-like situation".[^cite_note-212]

On 12 March, five United States Army soldiers of the [502nd Infantry Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/502nd_Infantry_Regiment) raped the 14-year-old Iraqi girl Abeer Qassim Hamza al-Janabi, and then murdered her, her father, her mother Fakhriya Taha Muhasen, and her six-year-old sister Hadeel Qassim Hamza al-Janabi. The soldiers then set fire to the girl's body to conceal evidence of the crime.[^cite_note-213] Four of the soldiers were convicted of rape and murder and the fifth was convicted of lesser crimes for their involvement in the events, which became known as the [Mahmudiyah rape and killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmudiyah_rape_and_killings).[^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215]

On 6 June 2006, the US successfully tracked [Abu Musab al-Zarqawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Musab_al-Zarqawi), the leader of [al-Qaeda in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_Iraq) who was killed in a [targeted killing](33928336.md), while attending a meeting in an isolated safehouse approximately 8 km (5.0 mi) north of Baqubah. Having been tracked by a British UAV, radio contact was made between the controller and two US Air Force [F-16](11642.md)C jets, which identified the house and at 14\:15 GMT, the lead jet dropped two 500‑pound (230 kg) guided bombs, a laser-guided GBU‑12 and GPS-guided GBU‑38 on the building where he was located. Six others – three male and three female individuals – were also reported killed. Among those killed were one of his wives and their child.

The government of Iraq took office on 20 May 2006, following approval by the [members](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Members_of_the_1st_Iraqi_Council_of_Representatives) of the [Iraqi National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_National_Assembly). This followed the [general election in December 2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/December_2005_Iraqi_legislative_election). The government succeeded the Iraqi Transitional Government, which had continued in office in a [caretaker capacity](240683.md) until the formation of the permanent government.

##### Iraq Study Group report and Saddam's execution

Main articles: [Iraq Study Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Study_Group) and [Execution of Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Execution_of_Saddam_Hussein)

The [Iraq Study Group Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Study_Group_Report) was released on 6 December 2006. The Iraq Study Group made up of people from both of the major US parties, was led by co-chairs [James Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Baker), a former Secretary of State (Republican), and [Lee H. Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_H._Hamilton), a former US Representative (Democrat). It concluded that "the situation in Iraq is grave and deteriorating" and "US forces seem to be caught in a mission that has no foreseeable end." The report's 79 recommendations include increasing diplomatic measures with [Iran](14653.md) and [Syria](7515849.md) and intensifying efforts to train Iraqi troops. On 18 December, a Pentagon report found that insurgent attacks were averaging about 960 attacks per week, the highest since the reports had begun in 2005.[^cite_note-216]

Coalition forces formally transferred control of a governorate to the Iraqi government. Military prosecutors charged eight US Marines with the murders of 24 Iraqi civilians in [Haditha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haditha) in November 2005, 10 of them women and children. Four officers were also charged with [dereliction of duty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dereliction_of_duty) in relation to the event.[^cite_note-217]

Saddam Hussein was hanged on 30 December 2006, after being found guilty of [crimes against humanity](61099.md) by an Iraqi court after a year-long trial.[^cite_note-218]

#### 2007: US troops surge

Further information: [2007 in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_in_Iraq), [Iraq War troop surge of 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_troop_surge_of_2007), [Timeline of the Iraq War troop surge of 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Iraq_War_troop_surge_of_2007), and [Strategic reset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_reset)



President [George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_W._Bush) announces the new strategy on Iraq from the White House Library, 10 January 2007

On 10 January 2007, in a televised address to the US public, Bush proposed 21,500 more troops for Iraq, a job program for Iraqis, more reconstruction proposals, and \$1.2 billion for these programs.[^cite_note-219] On 23 January 2007, in the [2007 State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_State_of_the_Union_Address), Bush announced he was "deploying reinforcements of more than 20,000 additional soldiers and Marines to Iraq". On 10 February 2007, [David Petraeus](3223944.md) was made commander of [Multi-National Force – Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multinational_Force_Iraq) (MNF-I), the four-star post that oversees all coalition forces in the country, replacing General [George Casey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_William_Casey_Jr.). In his new position, Petraeus oversaw all coalition forces in Iraq and employed them in the new ["Surge" strategy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_troop_surge_of_2007) outlined by the Bush administration.[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221]



&#x20;Map of the [Islamic State of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq) and its provinces on 7 April 2007

On 10 May 2007, 144 Iraqi Parliamentary lawmakers signed onto a legislative petition calling on the US to set a timetable for withdrawal.[^cite_note-222] On 3 June 2007, the Iraqi Parliament voted 85 to 59 to require the Iraqi government to consult with Parliament before requesting additional extensions of the UN Security Council Mandate for coalition operations in Iraq.[^cite_note-223]

Pressures on US troops were compounded by continued withdrawal of coalition forces.[^cite_note-\:4-224] In early 2007, [British Prime Minister](24150.md) Blair announced that following [Operation Sinbad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Sinbad), British troops would begin to withdraw from [Basra Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basra_Governorate), handing security over to the Iraqis.[^cite_note-\:4-224] In July Danish Prime Minister [Anders Fogh Rasmussen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anders_Fogh_Rasmussen) also announced the withdrawal of 441 Danish troops from Iraq, leaving only a unit of nine soldiers manning four observational helicopters.[^cite_note-225] In October 2019, the new Danish government said it would not re-open an official probe into the country's participation in the US-led military coalition in 2003 Iraq War.[^cite_note-226]

##### Planned troop reduction

In a speech made to Congress on 10 September 2007, [Petraeus](3223944.md) "envisioned the withdrawal of roughly 30,000 US troops by next summer, beginning with a Marine contingent \[in September]."[^cite_note-227] On 13 September, Bush announced a limited withdrawal of troops from Iraq.[^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229]

Bush said 5,700 personnel would be home by Christmas 2007, and expected thousands more to return by July 2008. The plan would take troop numbers back to their level before the surge at the beginning of 2007.

##### Effects of the surge on security

By March 2008, violence in Iraq was reportedly curtailed by 40–80%, according to a Pentagon report.[^cite_note-230] Independent reports[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232] raised questions about those assessments. An Iraqi military spokesman claimed that civilian deaths since the start of the troop surge plan were 265 in Baghdad, down from 1,440 in the four previous weeks. *[The New York Times](30680.md)* counted more than 450 Iraqi civilians killed during the same 28‑day period, based on initial daily reports from [Iraqi Interior Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Interior_Ministry) and hospital officials.

&#x20;\
&#x20;Historically, the daily counts tallied by *The New York Times* underestimated the total death toll by 50% or more when compared to studies by the UN, which rely upon figures from the [Iraqi Health Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Health_Ministry) and morgue figures.[^cite_note-233]

The rate of US combat deaths in Baghdad nearly doubled to 3.14 per day in the first seven weeks of the "surge" in security activity, compared to the previous period. Across the rest of Iraq, it decreased slightly.[^cite_note-234][^cite_note-235]

On 14 August 2007, the [deadliest single attack of the whole war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Qahtaniya_bombings) occurred. Nearly 800 civilians were killed by a series of coordinated suicide bomb attacks on the northern Iraqi settlement of [Kahtaniya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahtaniya). Over 100 homes and shops were destroyed in the blast. US officials blamed al‑Qaeda. The targeted villagers belonged to the non-Muslim [Yazidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazidi) ethnic minority. The attack may have represented the latest in a feud that erupted earlier that year when members of the Yazidi community stoned to death a teenage girl called [Du'a Khalil Aswad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Du'a_Khalil_Aswad) accused of dating a Sunni Arab man and converting to Islam. The killing of the girl was recorded on camera-mobiles and the video was uploaded onto the internet.[^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239]

On 13 September 2007, [Abdul Sattar Abu Risha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Sattar_Abu_Risha) was killed in a bomb attack in the city of [Ramadi](245655.md).[^cite_note-240] He was an important US ally because he led the "[Anbar Awakening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anbar_Awakening)", an alliance of Sunni Arab tribes that opposed al-Qaeda. The latter organization claimed responsibility for the attack.[^cite_note-241] A statement posted on the Internet by the shadowy [Islamic State of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq) called Abu Risha "one of the dogs of Bush" and described Thursday's killing as a "heroic operation that took over a month to prepare".[^cite_note-242]



A graph of US troop fatalities in Iraq from March 2003 to July 2010, the orange and blue months are the period of the [troop surge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_troop_surge_of_2007) and its aftermath

There was a reported trend of decreasing US troop deaths after May 2007, and violence against coalition troops had fallen to the "lowest levels since the first year of the American invasion".[^cite_note-243] These, and several other positive developments, were attributed to the surge by many analysts.[^cite_note-244]

Data from the Pentagon and other US agencies such as the [Government Accountability Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Accountability_Office) (GAO) found that daily attacks against civilians in Iraq remained "about the same" since February. The GAO also stated that there was no discernible trend in sectarian violence.[^cite_note-245] This report ran counter to reports to Congress, which showed a general downward trend in civilian deaths and ethno-sectarian violence since December 2006.[^cite_note-246] By late 2007, as the US troop surge began to wind down, violence in Iraq had begun to decrease from its 2006 highs.[^cite_note-247]

Entire neighborhoods in Baghdad were ethnically cleansed by Shia and Sunni militias and [sectarian violence](648554.md) broke out in every Iraqi city where there was a mixed population.[^cite_note-guardian2007-248][^cite_note-independent1-249][^cite_note-ahram2006-250] Investigative reporter [Bob Woodward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Woodward) cited US government sources according to which the US "surge" was not the primary reason for the drop in violence in 2007–08. Instead, according to that view, the reduction of violence was due to newer covert techniques by US military and intelligence officials to find, target, and kill insurgents, including working closely with former insurgents.[^cite_note-251]

In the Shia region near [Basra](57580.md), British forces turned over security for the region to Iraqi Security Forces. Basra was the ninth governorate of Iraq's 18 governorates to be returned to local security forces' control since the beginning of the occupation.[^cite_note-252]

##### Political developments

Over half of the members of Iraq's parliament rejected the continuing occupation of their country for the first time. 144 of the 275 lawmakers signed onto a legislative petition that would require the Iraqi government to seek approval from Parliament before it requests an extension of the UN mandate for foreign forces to be in Iraq, which expires at the end of 2008. It also calls for a timetable for troop withdrawal and a freeze on the size of foreign forces. The UN Security Council mandate for US‑led forces in Iraq will terminate "if requested by the government of Iraq."[^cite_note-253] 59% of those polled in the US support a timetable for withdrawal.[^cite_note-254]

In mid-2007, the coalition began a controversial program to recruit Iraqi Sunnis (often former insurgents) for the formation of "Guardian" militias. These Guardian militias are intended to support and secure various Sunni neighborhoods against the Islamists.[^cite_note-255]

##### Tensions with Iran

Further information: [Iran–United States relations during the G.W. Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93United_States_relations_during_the_G.W._Bush_administration) and [Karbala provincial headquarters raid](9129813.md)

In 2007, tensions increased greatly between [Iran](14653.md) and [Iraqi Kurdistan](679693.md) due to the latter's giving sanctuary to the militant Kurdish secessionist group [Party for a Free Life in Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan_Free_Life_Party) (PEJAK). According to reports, Iran had been shelling PEJAK positions in Iraqi Kurdistan since 16 August. These tensions further increased with a border incursion on 23 August by Iranian troops who attacked several Kurdish villages killing an unknown number of civilians and militants.[^cite_note-256]

Coalition forces also [began to target](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_kill_or_capture_strategy_in_Iraq) alleged Iranian [Quds force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quds_force) operatives in Iraq, either [arresting or killing suspected members](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._raid_on_the_Iranian_Liaison_Office_in_Erbil). The Bush administration and coalition leaders began to publicly state that Iran was supplying weapons, particularly [EFP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Explosively_formed_penetrator) devices, to Iraqi insurgents and militias although to date have failed to provide any proof for these allegations. Further sanctions on Iranian organizations were also announced by the Bush administration in autumn 2007. On 21 November 2007, Lieutenant General James Dubik, who is in charge of training Iraqi security forces, praised Iran for its "contribution to the reduction of violence" in Iraq by upholding its pledge to stop the flow of weapons, explosives, and training of extremists in Iraq.[^cite_note-257]

##### Tensions with Turkey

Further information: [2008 Turkish incursion into northern Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Turkish_incursion_into_northern_Iraq)

Border incursions by [PKK](69680.md) militants based in Northern Iraq have continued to harass Turkish forces, with casualties on both sides. In the fall of 2007, the Turkish military stated their right to cross the Iraqi Kurdistan border in "hot pursuit" of PKK militants and began shelling Kurdish areas in Iraq and attacking PKK bases in the [Mount Cudi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Cudi) region with aircraft.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259] The Turkish parliament approved a resolution permitting the military to pursue the PKK in Iraqi Kurdistan.[^cite_note-260] In November, Turkish gunships attacked parts of northern Iraq in the first such attack by Turkish aircraft since the border tensions escalated.[^cite_note-261] Another series of attacks in mid-December hit PKK targets in the Qandil, Zap, Avashin and Hakurk regions. The latest series of attacks involved at least 50 aircraft and artillery and Kurdish officials reported one civilian killed and two wounded.[^cite_note-262]

Additionally, weapons that were given to Iraqi security forces by the US military were being recovered by authorities in Turkey after being used by PKK in that state.[^cite_note-263]

##### Blackwater private security controversy

Main article: [Nisour Square massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nisour_Square_massacre)

On 17 September 2007, the Iraqi government announced that it was revoking the license of the US security firm [Blackwater USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackwater_USA) over the firm's involvement in the killing of eight civilians, including a woman and an infant,[^cite_note-264] in a firefight that followed a car bomb explosion near a State Department motorcade.

#### 2008: Civil war continues

Further information: [2008 in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_in_Iraq) and [Iraqi civil war (2006–2008)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_civil_war_\(2006%E2%80%932008\))



An Iraqi Army battalion training for urban operations

Throughout 2008, US officials and independent think tanks began to point to improvements in the security situation, as measured by key statistics. According to the [US Defense Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Defense_Department), in December 2008 the "overall level of violence" in the country had dropped 80% since before [the surge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_troop_surge_of_2007) began in January 2007, and the country's murder rate had dropped to prewar levels. They also pointed out that the casualty figure for US forces in 2008 was 314 against a figure of 904 in 2007.[^cite_note-DecDefLink-265]

According to the [Brookings Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookings_Institution), Iraqi civilian fatalities numbered 490 in November 2008 as against 3,500 in January 2007, whereas attacks against the coalition numbered somewhere between 200 and 300 per week in the latter half of 2008, as opposed to a peak of nearly 1,600 in summer 2007. The number of Iraqi security forces killed was under 100 per month in the second half of 2008, from a high of 200 to 300 in the summer of 2007.[^cite_note-266]

Meanwhile, the proficiency of the Iraqi military increased as it launched a spring offensive against Shia militias, which Prime Minister [Nouri al-Maliki](4723094.md) had previously been criticized for allowing to operate. This began with a March [operation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Basra_\(2008\)) against the [Mahdi Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahdi_Army) in Basra, which led to fighting in Shia areas up and down the country, especially in the [Sadr City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadr_City) district of Baghdad. By October, the British officer in charge of Basra said that since the operation, the town had become "secure" and had a murder rate comparable to [Manchester](20206.md) in England.[^cite_note-267] The US military also said there had been a decrease of about a quarter in the quantity of Iranian-made explosives found in Iraq in 2008, possibly indicating a change in Iranian policy.[^cite_note-268]
