Progress in Sunni areas continued after members of [the Awakening movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Awakening_movements_in_Iraq) were transferred from US military to Iraqi control.[^cite_note-269] In May, the Iraqi army – backed by coalition support – launched an offensive in [Mosul](200475.md), the last major Iraqi stronghold of al-Qaeda. Despite detaining thousands of individuals, the offensive failed to lead to major long-term security improvements in Mosul. At the end of the year, the city remained a major flashpoint.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271]

In the regional dimension, the ongoing conflict between Turkey and [PKK](69680.md)[^cite_note-272][^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] intensified on 21 February, when Turkey [launched a ground attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Turkish_incursion_into_northern_Iraq) into the Quandeel Mountains of Northern Iraq. In the nine-day-long operation, around 10,000 Turkish troops advanced up to 25 km into Northern Iraq. This was the first substantial ground incursion by Turkish forces since 1995.[^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276]

Shortly after the incursion began, both the Iraqi cabinet and the Kurdistan regional government condemned Turkey's actions and called for the immediate withdrawal of Turkish troops from the region.[^cite_note-277] Turkish troops withdrew on 29 February.[^cite_note-278] The fate of the Kurds and the future of the ethnically diverse city of [Kirkuk](199292.md) remained a contentious issue in Iraqi politics.

US military officials met these trends with cautious optimism as they approached what they described as the "transition" embodied in the [US–Iraq Status of Forces Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US%E2%80%93Iraq_Status_of_Forces_Agreement), which was negotiated throughout 2008.[^cite_note-DecDefLink-265] The commander of the coalition, US General [Raymond T. Odierno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raymond_T._Odierno), noted that "in military terms, transitions are the most dangerous time" in December 2008.[^cite_note-DecDefLink-265]

##### Spring offensives on Shiite militias



An Iraqi soldier and vehicles from the 42nd  Brigade, 11th Iraqi Army Division during a firefight with armed militiamen in the Sadr City district of Baghdad 17 April 2008

Further information: [2008 Iraq spring fighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Iraq_spring_fighting), [Siege of Sadr City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Sadr_City), and [Battle of Basra (2008)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Basra_\(2008\))

At the end of March, the Iraqi Army, with coalition air support, launched an offensive, dubbed "Charge of the Knights", in Basra to secure the area from militias. This was the first major operation where the Iraqi Army did not have direct combat support from conventional coalition ground troops. The offensive was opposed by the [Mahdi Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahdi_Army), one of the militias, which controlled much of the region.[^cite_note-279][^cite_note-driveinbasra-280] Fighting quickly spread to other parts of Iraq: including [Sadr City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadr_City), [Al Kut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Kut), [Al Hillah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Hillah) and others. During the fighting Iraqi forces met stiff resistance from militiamen in Basra to the point that the Iraqi military offensive slowed to a crawl, with the high attrition rates finally forcing the Sadrists to the negotiating table.

Following intercession by the Iranian government, al‑Sadr ordered a ceasefire on 30 March 2008.[^cite_note-281] The militiamen kept their weapons.

By 12 May 2008, Basra "residents overwhelmingly reported a substantial improvement in their everyday lives" according to *[The New York Times](30680.md)*. "Government forces have now taken over Islamic militants' headquarters and halted the death squads and 'vice enforcers' who attacked women, Christians, musicians, alcohol sellers and anyone suspected of collaborating with Westerners", according to the report; however, when asked how long it would take for lawlessness to resume if the Iraqi army left, one resident replied, "one day".[^cite_note-driveinbasra-280]

In late April roadside bombings continued to rise from a low in January – from 114 bombings to more than 250, surpassing the May 2007 high.

##### Congressional testimony



General [David Petraeus](3223944.md) in testimony before Congress on 8 April 2008

Speaking before Congress on 8 April 2008, General [David Petraeus](3223944.md) urged delaying troop withdrawals, saying, "I've repeatedly noted that we haven't turned any corners, we haven't seen any lights at the end of the tunnel," referencing the comments of then-President Bush and former Vietnam-era General [William Westmoreland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Westmoreland).[^cite_note-282] When asked by the Senate if reasonable people could disagree on the way forward, Petraeus said, "We fight for the right of people to have other opinions."[^cite_note-283]

Upon questioning by then Senate committee chair [Joe Biden](145422.md), Ambassador Crocker admitted that [Al‑Qaeda](1921.md) in Iraq was less important than the Al Qaeda organization led by [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden) along the Afghan-Pakistani border.[^cite_note-284] Lawmakers from both parties complained that US taxpayers are carrying Iraq's burden as it earns billions of dollars in oil revenues.

##### Iraqi security forces rearm

Iraq became one of the top purchasers of US military equipment with their army trading its [AK‑47](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AK-47) assault rifles for the US [M‑16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M16_rifle) and [M‑4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M4_carbine) rifles, among other equipment.[^cite_note-285] In 2008 alone, Iraq accounted for more than \$12.5 billion of the \$34 billion US weapon sales to foreign countries (not including the potential F-16 fighter planes.).[^cite_note-286]

Iraq sought 36 [F‑16s](11642.md), the most sophisticated weapons system Iraq has attempted to purchase. The Pentagon notified Congress that it had approved the sale of 24 American attack helicopters to Iraq, valued at as much as \$2.4 billion. Including the helicopters, Iraq announced plans to purchase at least \$10 billion in US tanks and armored vehicles, transport planes, and other battlefield equipment and services. Over the summer, the Defense Department announced that the Iraqi government wanted to order more than 400 armored vehicles and other equipment worth up to \$3 billion, and six C-130J transport planes, worth up to \$1.5 billion.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288] From 2005 to 2008, the United States had completed approximately \$20 billion in arms sales agreements with Iraq.[^cite_note-289]

##### Status of forces agreement

Main article: [US–Iraq Status of Forces Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US%E2%80%93Iraq_Status_of_Forces_Agreement)

The [US–Iraq Status of Forces Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US%E2%80%93Iraq_Status_of_Forces_Agreement) was approved by the Iraqi government on 4 December 2008.[^cite_note-Zawya-290] It established that US combat forces would withdraw from Iraqi cities by 30 June 2009, and that all US forces would be completely out of Iraq by 31 December 2011. The pact was subject to possible negotiations which could have delayed withdrawal and a referendum scheduled for mid-2009 in Iraq, which might have required all US forces to completely leave by the middle of 2010.[^cite_note-bbcsofa-291][^cite_note-292] The pact required criminal charges for holding prisoners over 24 hours, and required a warrant for searches of homes and buildings that are not related to combat.[^cite_note-sofatext-293]



Street fighting in [Mosul](200475.md) in January 2008

US contractors working for US forces were to be subject to Iraqi criminal law, while contractors working for the State Department and other US agencies may retain their immunity. If US forces commit still undecided "major premeditated felonies" while off-duty and off-base, they will be subject to the still undecided procedures laid out by a joint US‑Iraq committee if the United States certifies the forces were off-duty.[^cite_note-Juris-294][^cite_note-JMOCC-295][^cite_note-autogenerated1-296][^cite_note-ihtsofa-297]

Some Americans have discussed "loopholes"[^cite_note-Silent-298] and some Iraqis have said they believe parts of the pact remain a "mystery".[^cite_note-WPSist-299] US Secretary of Defense Robert Gates predicted that after 2011 he expected to see "perhaps several tens of thousands of American troops" as part of a residual force in Iraq.[^cite_note-GatesResidual-300]

Several groups of Iraqis protested the passing of the SOFA accord[^cite_note-SadrProtests-301][^cite_note-AMS-302][^cite_note-Refugees-303] as prolonging and legitimizing the occupation. Tens of thousands of Iraqis burned an [effigy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effigy) of [George W. Bush](3414021.md) in a [central Baghdad square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firdos_Square) where US troops five years previously organized a tearing down of a statue of Saddam Hussein.[^cite_note-Los_Angeles_Times-170][^cite_note-WPSist-299][^cite_note-aljazeera.com-304] Some Iraqis expressed skeptical optimism that the US would completely end its presence by 2011.[^cite_note-IHT_Mixed-305] On 4 December 2008, Iraq's presidential council approved the security pact.[^cite_note-Zawya-290]

A representative of Grand Ayatollah Ali Husseini al‑Sistani expressed concern with the ratified version of the pact and noted that the government of Iraq has no authority to control the transfer of occupier forces into and out of Iraq, no control of shipments and that the pact grants the occupiers immunity from prosecution in Iraqi courts. He said that Iraqi rule in the country is not complete while the occupiers are present, but that ultimately the Iraqi people would judge the pact in a referendum.[^cite_note-aljazeera.com-304] Thousands of Iraqis have gathered weekly after Friday prayers and shouted anti‑US and anti-Israeli slogans protesting the security pact between Baghdad and Washington. A protester said that despite the approval of the Interim Security pact, the Iraqi people would break it in a referendum next year.[^cite_note-306]

#### 2009: Coalition redeployment

Further information: [2009 in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_in_Iraq)

##### Transfer of the Green Zone



Aerial view of the [Green Zone](856542.md), Baghdad International Airport, and the contiguous Victory Base Complex in Baghdad

On 1 January 2009, the United States handed control of the [Green Zone](856542.md) and Saddam Hussein's presidential palace to the Iraqi government in a ceremonial move described by the country's prime minister as a restoration of Iraq's sovereignty. Iraqi Prime Minister Nouri al-Maliki said he would propose 1 January be declared national "Sovereignty Day". "This palace is the symbol of Iraqi sovereignty and by restoring it, a real message is directed to all Iraqi people that Iraqi sovereignty has returned to its natural status", al‑Maliki said.[^cite_note-307]

The US military attributed a decline in reported civilian deaths to several factors including the US‑led "troop surge", the growth of US-funded [Awakening Councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Awakening), and Shiite cleric Muqtada al-Sadr's call for his militia to abide by a cease fire.[^cite_note-308]

##### Provincial elections

Main article: [2009 Iraqi governorate elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Iraqi_governorate_elections)

On 31 January, Iraq held provincial elections.[^cite_note-309] Provincial candidates and those close to them faced some political assassinations and attempted assassinations, and there was also some other violence related to the election.[^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311][^cite_note-312]

Iraqi voter turnout failed to meet the original expectations which were set and was the lowest on record in Iraq,[^cite_note-ProvVote-313] but US Ambassador [Ryan Crocker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryan_Crocker) characterized the turnout as "large".[^cite_note-314] Of those who turned out to vote, some groups complained of disenfranchisement and fraud.[^cite_note-ProvVote-313][^cite_note-315][^cite_note-316] After the post-election curfew was lifted, some groups made threats about what would happen if they were unhappy with the results.[^cite_note-317]

##### Exit strategy announcement

US President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) delivering a speech at Camp Lejeune on 27 February 2009

On 27 February, United States President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) gave a speech at [Marine Corps Base Camp Lejeune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Corps_Base_Camp_Lejeune) in the US state of [North Carolina](21650.md) announcing that the US combat mission in Iraq would end by 31 August 2010. A "transitional force" of up to 50,000 troops tasked with training the [Iraqi Security Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Security_Forces), conducting [counterterrorism](83392.md) operations, and providing general support may remain until the end of 2011, the president added. However, the insurgency in 2011 and the rise of ISIL in 2014 caused the war to continue.[^cite_note-318]

The day before Obama's speech, Prime Minister of Iraq [Nouri al‑Maliki](4723094.md) said at a press conference that the [government of Iraq](11752991.md) had "no worries" over the impending departure of US forces and expressed confidence in the ability of the Iraqi Security Forces and police to maintain order without US military support.[^cite_note-319]

##### Sixth anniversary protests

On 9 April, the 6th anniversary of Baghdad's fall to coalition forces, tens of thousands of Iraqis thronged Baghdad to mark the anniversary and demand the immediate departure of coalition forces. The crowds of Iraqis stretched from the Sadr City slum in northeast Baghdad to the square around 5 km (3.1 mi) away, where protesters burned an effigy featuring the face of US President George W. Bush.[^cite_note-320] There were also Sunni Muslims in the crowd. Police said many Sunnis, including prominent leaders such as a founding sheikh from the [Sons of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sons_of_Iraq), took part.[^cite_note-MH040909-321]

##### Coalition forces withdraw

On 30 April, the United Kingdom formally ended combat operations. Prime Minister [Gordon Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gordon_Brown) characterized the operation in Iraq as a "success story" because of UK troops' efforts. Britain handed control of Basra to the United States Armed Forces.[^cite_note-322]

The withdrawal of US forces began at the end of June, with 38 bases to be handed over to Iraqi forces. On 29 June 2009, US forces withdrew from Baghdad. On 30 November 2009, Iraqi [Interior Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Interior_\(Iraq\)) officials reported that the civilian death toll in Iraq fell to its lowest level in November since the 2003 invasion.[^cite_note-323]

On 28 July, Australia withdrew its combat forces as the Australian military presence in Iraq ended, per an agreement with the Iraqi government.

##### Iraq awards oil contracts



[US Navy](20518076.md) and Coast Guard personnel stand guard aboard the [Al Basrah Oil Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Basrah_Oil_Terminal) in July 2009

On 30 June and 11 December 2009, the [Iraqi ministry of oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Oil_\(Iraq\)) awarded contracts to international oil companies for some of [Iraq's many oil fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Iraq#2009_Oil_services_contracts). The winning oil companies entered joint ventures with the Iraqi ministry of oil, and the terms of the awarded contracts included extraction of oil for a fixed fee of approximately \$1.40 per barrel.[^cite_note-aljazeera091211-324][^cite_note-aljazeera090630-325][^cite_note-iraq123_news-326] The fees will only be paid once a production threshold set by the Iraqi ministry of oil is reached.

#### 2010: US drawdown and Operation New Dawn&#x20;

Further information: [2010 in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_in_Iraq) and [Withdrawal of United States troops from Iraq (2007–2011)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_United_States_troops_from_Iraq_\(2007%E2%80%932011\))

On 17 February 2010, US Secretary of Defense [Robert Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Gates) announced that as of 1 September, the name "Operation Iraqi Freedom" would be replaced by "Operation New Dawn".[^cite_note-327]

On 18 April, US and Iraqi forces killed [Abu Ayyub al-Masri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ayyub_al-Masri), the leader of [al-Qaeda in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_Iraq) in a joint American and Iraqi operation near [Tikrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tikrit), Iraq.[^cite_note-328] The coalition forces believed al-Masri to be wearing a suicide vest and proceeded cautiously. After the lengthy exchange of fire and bombing of the house, the Iraqi troops stormed inside and found two women still alive, one of whom was al-Masri's wife, and four dead men, identified as al-Masri, [Abu Abdullah al-Rashid al-Baghdadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Abdullah_al-Rashid_al-Baghdadi), an assistant to al-Masri, and al-Baghdadi's son. A suicide vest was indeed found on al-Masri's corpse, as the Iraqi Army subsequently stated.[^cite_note-329] Iraqi Prime Minister [Nouri al-Maliki](4723094.md) announced the killings of Abu Omar al-Baghdadi and Abu Ayyub al-Masri at a news conference in Baghdad and showed reporters photographs of their bloody corpses. "The attack was carried out by ground forces which surrounded the house, and also through the use of missiles," Maliki said. "During the operation computers were seized with e-mails and messages to the two biggest terrorists, Osama bin Laden and \[his deputy] Ayman al-Zawahiri", Maliki added. US forces commander Gen. [Raymond Odierno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raymond_Odierno) praised the operation. "The death of these terrorists is potentially the most significant blow to al‑Qaeda in Iraq since the beginning of the insurgency", he said. "There is still work to do but this is a significant step forward in ridding Iraq of terrorists."

On 20 June, Iraq's Central Bank was bombed in an attack that left 15 people dead and brought much of downtown Baghdad to a standstill. The attack was claimed to have been carried out by the [Islamic State of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq). This attack was followed by another attack on Iraq's Bank of Trade building that killed 26 and wounded 52 people.[^cite_note-330]



Iraqi [commandos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commandos) training under the supervision of soldiers from the US [82nd Airborne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/82nd_Airborne) in December 2010

In late August 2010, insurgents conducted [a major attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/25_August_2010_Iraq_bombings) with at least 12 car bombs simultaneously detonating from Mosul to Basra and killing at least 51. These attacks coincided with the US plans for a withdrawal of combat troops.[^cite_note-331]

From the end of August 2010, the United States attempted to dramatically cut its combat role in Iraq, with the withdrawal of all US ground forces designated for active combat operations. [The last US combat brigades departed Iraq in the early morning of 19 August](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_U.S._troops_from_Iraq_\(2007%E2%80%932011\)#The_events_of_August_19,_2010). Convoys of US troops had been moving out of Iraq to [Kuwait](7515890.md) for several days, and [NBC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC_News) broadcast live from Iraq as the last convoy crossed the border. While all combat brigades left the country, an additional 50,000 personnel (including Advise and Assist Brigades) remained in the country to provide support for the Iraqi military.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333] These troops were required to leave Iraq by 31 December 2011 under an [agreement between the US and Iraqi governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S.%E2%80%93Iraq_Status_of_Forces_Agreement).[^cite_note-334]

The desire to step back from an active counter-insurgency role did not however mean that the Advise and Assist Brigades and other remaining US forces would not be caught up in combat. A standards memo from the Associated Press reiterated "combat in Iraq is not over, and we should not uncritically repeat suggestions that it is, even if they come from senior officials".[^cite_note-335]

State Department spokesman P. J. Crowley stated "... we are not ending our work in Iraq, We have a long-term commitment to Iraq."[^cite_note-336] On 31 August, from the Oval Office, Barack Obama announced his intent to end the combat mission in Iraq. In his address, he covered the role of the United States' soft power, the effect the war had on the United States economy, and the legacy of the Afghanistan and Iraq wars.[^cite_note-337]

On the same day in Iraq, at a ceremony at one of [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein)'s former residences at [Al-Faw Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Faw_Palace) in Baghdad, a number of US dignitaries spoke in a ceremony for television cameras, avoiding overtones of the [triumphalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Mission_Accomplished_Speech) present in US announcements made earlier in the war. Vice President [Joe Biden](145422.md) expressed concerns regarding the ongoing lack of progress in forming a new Iraqi government, saying of the Iraqi people that "they expect a government that reflects the results of the votes they cast". Gen. [Ray Odierno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ray_Odierno) stated that the new era "in no way signals the end of our commitment to the people of Iraq". Speaking in [Ramadi](245655.md) earlier in the day, Gates said that US forces "have accomplished something really quite extraordinary here, \[but] how it all weighs in the balance over time I think remains to be seen". When asked by reporters if the seven-year war was worth doing, Gates commented that "It really requires a historian's perspective in terms of what happens here in the long run". He noted the Iraq War "will always be clouded by how it began" regarding Saddam Hussein's supposed [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons_of_mass_destruction), which were never confirmed to have existed. Gates continued, "This is one of the reasons that this war remains so controversial at home".[^cite_note-338] On the same day Gen. [Ray Odierno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ray_Odierno) was replaced by [Lloyd Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloyd_Austin) as Commander of US forces in Iraq.



Alabama Army National Guard MP, MSG Schur, during a joint community policing patrol in Basra, 3 April 2010

On 7 September, two US troops were killed and nine wounded in an incident at an Iraqi military base. The incident is under investigation by Iraqi and US forces, but it is believed that an Iraqi soldier opened fire on US forces.[^cite_note-339]

On 8 September, the US Army announced the arrival in Iraq of the first specifically designated Advise and Assist Brigade, the [3d Armored Cavalry Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3d_Armored_Cavalry_Regiment). It was announced that the unit would assume responsibilities in five southern governorates.[^cite_note-340] From 10 to 13 September, the [25th Infantry Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/25th_Infantry_Division_\(United_States\))'s Second Advise and Assist Brigade [fought Iraqi insurgents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Palm_Grove) near [Diyala](377806.md).

According to reports from Iraq, hundreds of members of the Sunni [Awakening Councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sons_of_Iraq) may have switched allegiance back to the Iraqi insurgency or al-Qaeda.[^cite_note-341]

In October, [WikiLeaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WikiLeaks) disclosed [391,832 classified US military documents on the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_documents_leak).[^cite_note-spiegel-342][^cite_note-guardian-343][^cite_note-AlJazeera-344] Approximately, 58 people were killed with another 40 wounded in an attack on the [Sayidat al‑Nejat church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Baghdad_church_siege) in Baghdad. Responsibility for the attack was claimed by the Islamic State in Iraq organization.[^cite_note-345]

Coordinated attacks in primarily Shia areas struck throughout Baghdad on 2 November, killing approximately 113 and wounding 250 with around 17 bombs.[^cite_note-346]

##### Iraqi arms purchases



[M1 Abrams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M1_Abrams) tanks in Iraqi service, January 2011

As US forces departed the country, the [Iraq Defense Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_\(Iraq\)) solidified plans to purchase advanced military equipment from the United States. Plans in 2010 called for \$13 billion of purchases, to include 140 [M1 Abrams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M1_Abrams) main battle tanks.[^cite_note-347] In addition to the \$13 billion purchase, the Iraqis also requested 18 [F-16 Fighting Falcons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Dynamics_F%E2%80%9116_Fighting_Falcon) as part of a \$4.2 billion program that also included aircraft training and maintenance, [AIM‑9 Sidewinder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIM-9_Sidewinder) air-to-air missiles, [laser-guided bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laser-guided_bomb) and reconnaissance equipment.[^cite_note-autogenerated2-348] All Abrams tanks were delivered by the end of 2011,[^cite_note-349] but the first F-16s did not arrive in Iraq until 2015, due to concerns that the Islamic State might overrun [Balad Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balad_Air_Base).[^cite_note-350]

The Iraqi Navy also purchased 12 US‑built Swift-class patrol boats, at a cost of \$20 million each. Delivery was completed in 2013.[^cite_note-351] The vessels are used to protect the oil terminals at [Basra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Ba%C5%9Frah_Oil_Terminal) and [Khor al-Amiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khor_Al_Amaya_Oil_Terminal).[^cite_note-autogenerated2-348] Two US‑built offshore support vessels, each costing \$70 million, were delivered in 2011.[^cite_note-CordesmanKhazai2014-352]

##### The UN lifts restrictions on Iraq

In a move to legitimize the existing Iraqi government, the United Nations lifted the Saddam Hussein-era UN restrictions on Iraq. These included allowing Iraq to have a civilian nuclear program, permitting the participation of Iraq in international nuclear and chemical weapons treaties, as well as returning control of Iraq's oil and gas revenue to the government and ending the [Oil-for-Food Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil-for-Food_Programme).[^cite_note-353]

#### 2011: US withdrawal

Main article: [Withdrawal of United States troops from Iraq (2007–2011)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_United_States_troops_from_Iraq_\(2007%E2%80%932011\))

Further information: [2011 in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_in_Iraq)

Muqtada al-Sadr returned to Iraq in the holy city of Najaf to lead the Sadrist movement after being in exile since 2007.[^cite_note-354]



US Army soldier on the roof of an Iraqi police station in [Haqlaniyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haqlaniyah), July 2011

June 2011, became the bloodiest month in Iraq for the US military since June 2009, with 15 US soldiers killed, only one of them outside combat.[^cite_note-355]

On 7 July, two US troops were killed and one seriously injured in an IED attack at Victory Base Complex outside Baghdad. They were members of the 145th Brigade Support Battalion, 116th Cavalry Heavy Brigade Combat Team, an Idaho Army National Guard unit base in Post Falls, Idaho. Spc. Nathan R. Beyers, 24, and Spc. Nicholas W. Newby, 20, were killed in the attack, Staff Sgt. Jazon Rzepa, 30, was seriously injured.[^cite_note-356]

In September, Iraq signed a contract to buy 18 Lockheed Martin F-16 warplanes, becoming the 26th nation to operate the F-16. Because of windfall profits from oil, the Iraqi government is planning to double this originally planned 18, to 36 F-16s. Iraq is relying on the US military for air support as it rebuilds its forces and battles a stubborn Islamist insurgency.[^cite_note-357]

With the collapse of the discussions about extending the stay of any US troops beyond 2011, where they would not be granted any immunity from the Iraqi government, on 21 October 2011, President Obama announced at a White House press conference that all remaining US troops and trainers would leave Iraq by the end of the year as previously scheduled, bringing the US mission in Iraq to an end.[^cite_note-LeaveIn2011-358][^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360][^cite_note-pbs.org-361][^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363] The last American soldier to die in Iraq before the withdrawal, SPC. David Hickman, was killed by a roadside bomb in Baghdad on 14 November.[^cite_note-cnndec16-364]

In November 2011, the US Senate voted down a resolution to formally end the war by bringing its authorization by Congress to an end.[^cite_note-Antle_November_2011-365]



US and [Kuwaiti troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuwaiti_Army) closing the gate between Kuwait and Iraq on 18 December 2011

On 15 December, an American military ceremony was held in Baghdad putting a formal end to the US mission in Iraq.[^cite_note-366]

The last US combat troops withdrew from Iraq on 18 December 2011, although the US embassy and consulates continue to maintain a staff of more than 20,000 including 100+ military personnel within the Office of Security Cooperation-Iraq (OSC-I),[^cite_note-367] US [Marine Embassy Guards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Embassy_Guard) and between 4,000 and 5,000 [private military contractors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_military_company).[^cite_note-Denselow-368][^cite_note-369] The next day, Iraqi officials issued an arrest warrant for the Sunni Vice-president [Tariq al-Hashimi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tariq_al-Hashimi). He has been accused of involvement in assassinations and fled to the Kurdish part of Iraq.[^cite_note-370]

## Aftermath

### Emerging conflict and insurgency

Main articles: [Iraqi insurgency (2011–2013)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_insurgency_\(2011%E2%80%932013\)), [War in Iraq (2013–2017)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Iraq_\(2013%E2%80%932017\)), and [Islamic State insurgency in Iraq (2017–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_insurgency_in_Iraq_\(2017%E2%80%93present\))

Further information: [War against the Islamic State](43517689.md), [US-led intervention in Iraq (2014–2021)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US-led_intervention_in_Iraq_\(2014%E2%80%932021\)), and [Iranian intervention in Iraq (2014–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_intervention_in_Iraq_\(2014%E2%80%93present\))



June 2015 military situation:\


&#x20; Controlled by [Iraqi government](11752991.md)

&#x20; Controlled by the [Islamic State](9087364.md)

&#x20; Controlled by [Iraqi Kurds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshmerga)

&#x20; Controlled by [Syrian government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ministers_\(Syria\))

&#x20; Controlled by [Syrian rebels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_opposition)

&#x20; Controlled by [Syrian Kurds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_Supreme_Committee)

The invasion and occupation led to sectarian violence, which caused widespread displacement among Iraqi civilians. Since the beginning of the war, the first [parliamentary elections were held in 2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2005_Iraqi_parliamentary_election) which brought greater representation and autonomy to [Iraqi Kurds](35423251.md). By 2007 the Iraqi Red Crescent estimated 2.3 million Iraqis were internally displaced, with an estimated 2 million Iraqis fleeing to neighboring countries, mostly to Syria and Jordan.[^cite_note-371]

Sectarian violence continued in the first half of 2013. At least 56 people died in April when a Sunni protest in Hawija was interrupted by a government-supported helicopter raid and a series of violent incidents occurred in May. On 20 May 2013, at least 95 people died in a wave of car bomb attacks that was preceded by a car bombing on 15 May that led to 33 deaths; also, on 18 May 76 people were killed in the Sunni areas of Baghdad. Some experts have stated that Iraq could return to the brutal sectarian conflict of 2006.[^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373]

On 22 July 2013, at least five hundred convicts, most of whom were senior members of al-Qaeda who had received death sentences, were freed from Abu Ghraib jail in an insurgent attack, which began with a suicide bomb attack on the prison gates.[^cite_note-374] James F. Jeffrey, the United States ambassador in Baghdad when the last American troops exited, said the assault and resulting escape "will provide seasoned leadership and a morale boost to Al Qaeda and its allies in both Iraq and Syria ... it is likely to have an electrifying impact on the Sunni population in Iraq, which has been sitting on the fence."[^cite_note-375]

By mid-2014 Iraq was in chaos with a new government yet to be formed following national elections, and the insurgency reaching new heights. In early June 2014 the [Islamic State in Iraq and the Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_in_Iraq_and_the_Levant) (ISIL) took over the cities of Mosul and Tikrit and said it was ready to march on Baghdad, while Iraqi Kurdish forces took control of key military installations in the major oil city of Kirkuk. The al-Qaeda breakaway group formally declared the creation of an Islamic state on 29 June 2014, in the territory under its control.[^cite_note-376]

Prime Minister Nouri al-Maliki unsuccessfully asked his parliament to declare a [state of emergency](298608.md) that would give him increased powers.[^cite_note-IraqImploding-377] On 14 August 2014, Prime Minister Nouri al-Maliki succumbed to pressure at home and abroad to step down. This paved the way for [Haidar al-Abadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haidar_al-Abadi) to take over on 19 August 2014.

In September 2014, President Obama acknowledged that the US underestimated the rise of the Islamic State and overestimated the Iraqi military's ability to fight ISIL.[^cite_note-378] Obama announced the return of US forces, in the form of aerial support, in an effort to halt the advance of ISIL forces, render humanitarian aid to stranded refugees and stabilize the political situation.[^cite_note-AirSupport-379]

A [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Civil_War_\(2014%E2%80%932017\)) between ISIL and the central government continued for the next three years. Following the election of [Donald Trump](4848272.md), the United States intensified its campaign against the Islamic State by January 2017.[^cite_note-380] Defense Secretary [Jim Mattis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Mattis) said a tactical shift to surrounding Islamic State strongholds in Mosul, Iraq, and Raqqa, Syria, was devised not only to "annihilate" ISIL fighters hunkered down there, but also to prevent them from returning to their home nations in Europe, Africa, and the Middle East. In 2017, US-backed Kurdish forces captured [Raqqa](858965.md), which had served as the ISIL capital.[^cite_note-381] The Iraqi government declared victory against ISIL in December 2017.[^cite_note-end_of_war-382] By 2018, violence in Iraq was at its lowest level in ten years. This was largely a result of the defeat of ISIL forces and the subsequent calming-down of the [insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_insurgency_in_Iraq_\(2017%E2%80%93present\)).[^cite_note-383]

In January 2020, after the [assassination of Qasem Soleimani](62726668.md), the Iraqi parliament voted for all foreign troops to leave the country. This would end its standing agreement with the United States to station 5,200 soldiers in Iraq. Then-President Trump objected to withdrawing troops and threatened Iraq with sanctions over this decision.[^cite_note-384] In 2023, Iraqi Prime Minister [Mohammed Shia' Al Sudani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Shia'_Al_Sudani) indicated his support for an indefinite US military presence in Iraq.[^cite_note-385]

In September 2024, the United States and Iraq agreed to wind down the US-led coalition's military mission against the Islamic State in Iraq. On 30 September 2026, the last remaining US troops withdrew from Iraq, officially ending the US military mission in the country.[^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388]

## Casualties

### Casualty estimates

Main article: [Casualties of the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casualties_of_the_Iraq_War)

See also: [Foreign hostages in Iraq](2520764.md) and [List of bombings during the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_bombings_during_the_Iraq_War)



Wounded US personnel flown from Iraq to [Ramstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramstein_Air_Base), Germany, for medical treatment, February 2007

Gun camera footage of the [12 July 2007, Baghdad airstrike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_12,_2007,_Baghdad_airstrike), that killed 12 people, including [Reuters](18998750.md) employees [Namir Noor-Eldeen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namir_Noor-Eldeen) and [Saeed Chmagh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saeed_Chmagh)

For coalition death totals see the infobox at the top right. See also [Casualties of the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casualties_of_the_Iraq_War), which has casualty numbers for coalition nations, contractors, non-Iraqi civilians, journalists, media helpers, aid workers, and the wounded. Casualty figures, especially Iraqi ones, are highly disputed.

There have been several attempts by the media, coalition governments and others to estimate the Iraqi casualties. The table below summarizes some of these estimates and methods.

| Source | Iraqi casualties | March 2003 to ... |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **[Iraq Family Health Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Family_Health_Survey)** | **151,000** violent deaths | June 2006 |
| **[Lancet survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancet_surveys_of_Iraq_War_casualties)** | **601,027** violent deaths out of **654,965** excess deaths | June 2006 |
| **PLOS Medicine Study** | **460,000** excess deaths including **132,000** violent deaths from the conflict[^cite_note-Hagopian-90] | June 2011 |
| **[Opinion Research Business survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ORB_survey_of_Iraq_War_casualties)** | **1,033,000** violent deaths from the conflict | August 2007 |
| **[Iraqi Health Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Health_Ministry)** | **87,215** violent deaths per death certificates issued Deaths prior to January 2005 unrecorded Ministry estimates up to 20% more deaths are undocumented. | January 2005 to February 2009 |
| **[Associated Press](#Associated_Press)** | **110,600** violent deaths Health Ministry death certificates plus AP estimate of casualties for 2003–04 | April 2009 |
| **[Iraq Body Count](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Body_Count_project)** | **105,052–114,731** violent civilian deaths compiled from commercial news media, NGO and official reports Over **162,000** civilian and combatant deaths | January 2012 |
| **[WikiLeaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WikiLeaks). Classified [Iraq War Logs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_Logs)** | **109,032** violent deaths including 66,081 civilian deaths | January 2004 to December 2009 |

## Impacts

### Economic impact

#### Financial cost

Main article: [Financial cost of the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_cost_of_the_Iraq_War)



A US Army soldier watching a burning oil well at [Rumaila oil field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumaila_oil_field) in April 2003; the fire was later extinguished by coalition personnel

In 2013, the total cost of the war to date was estimated at \$1.7 trillion by the [Watson Institute for International and Public Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watson_Institute_for_International_and_Public_Affairs) at [Brown University](4157.md).[^cite_note-389] However, some economists argue the total cost to the US economy could range from \$3[^cite_note-390] to \$6 trillion, including long-term interest and veterans' costs, by 2053.[^cite_note-391] The upper ranges of these estimates include the long-term cost of disability compensation and medical care to US troops. Harvard's public finance expert, Linda J. Bilmes, estimated that these costs alone would amount to nearly \$1 trillion over the next 40 years.[^cite_note-392] Bilmes also argued the war diverted resources from Afghanistan, raised oil prices, increased US federal debt, and contributed to the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis).[^cite_note-393] The most recent estimates indicate costs will exceed \$2.9 trillion by 2050. This figure includes direct costs such as military operations, veterans' care, and reconstruction, as well as long-term expenses, particularly for veterans' healthcare and disability benefits. As of 2023, \$1.8 trillion had been spent, and costs will continue over the coming decades due to care for veterans and other war-related expenditures.[^cite_note-394]

A [CNN](62028.md) report noted that the US-led interim government, the [Coalition Provisional Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Provisional_Authority) (CPA), lost track of \$8.8 billion from the [Development Fund for Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Development_Fund_for_Iraq) during its tenure.[^cite_note-395] Additionally, in 2011, CBS News reported that \$6 billion in cash, was airlifted into Iraq by the Bush administration aboard military cargo planes. This was part of a total of \$12 billion sent in cash over 21 separate flights by May 2004, much of which disappeared.[^cite_note-396] Stuart Bowen, director of the [Office of the Special Inspector General for Iraq Reconstruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_the_Special_Inspector_General_for_Iraq_Reconstruction), stated that the CPA had failed to establish sufficient controls to ensure the funds were used transparently, adding that the missing money might represent "the largest theft of funds in national history."[^cite_note-397]

#### Reparations

By 2013, some human rights groups in both Iraq and the US had begun campaigning for [reparations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_reparations) from the US for the devastation and health effects suffered by Iraqis during the war.[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399]

### Humanitarian impact

#### Humanitarian crisis

Main articles: [Humanitarian crises of the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanitarian_crises_of_the_Iraq_War) and [Refugees of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_Iraq)



Child killed by a car bomb in Kirkuk, July 2011

According to a 2007 Oxfam report, the child [malnutrition](258979.md) rate had risen to 28%, and the rate of people without access to clean drinking water had risen to 70%.[^cite_note-400] In 2007, Nasser Muhssin, a researcher on family and children's affairs affiliated to the University of Baghdad claimed that 60–70% of Iraqi children suffered from psychological problems.[^cite_note-401] A [2007 cholera outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Iraq_cholera_outbreak) in northern Iraq was thought to be the result of poor water quality.[^cite_note-402] As many as half of Iraqi doctors left the country between 2003 and 2006.[^cite_note-403]

### Environmental impact

#### Oil pollution

The war has led to [oil spills](158705.md), which increased carbon emissions and contaminated the surrounding water resources. During the invasion period, the retreating [Iraqi army](1576797.md) damaged the oil infrastructure and destroyed more than 736 oil wells in southern Iraq, resulting in fires and massive oil spills.[^cite_note-\:1-404] In 2003, more than 50 billion tonnes of carbon emissions were produced from burning oil fields.[^cite_note-405] Over 130 million gallons of oil leaked into surrounding water resources, such as [Sawa Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sawa_Lake).[^cite_note-\:2-406] Between 2003 and 2010, more than 5,000 birds from three species died around Sawa Lake.[^cite_note-\:2-406]

#### Radioactive contamination



The US army testing the harmful radiation fragments on the ground in Basra, Iraq

The US-led coalition used [depleted uranium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depleted_uranium) (DU) munitions during the war to pierce tank armour.[^cite_note-407] 1,000 to 2,000 tonnes (980 to 1,970 long tons; 1,100 to 2,200 short tons) of DU munitions were fired, which caused ammunition fragments containing radioactive material to spread across the country. According to a [United Nations Environment Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Environment_Programme) report, radioactive material contaminated air and soil; with the radioactive concentration found in Iraqi soil at 709.52 Bq in 2003 compared to 143.22 Bq in 2002.[^cite_note-\:3-408] The report states that high levels of radiation prevented plants, especially crop seeds, from sprouting; with about 22% (9.5 million ha) of the farmland in Iraq unable to grow barley.[^cite_note-\:2-406]

In addition, radiation contamination may have had harmful public health outcomes through poisoning and increased incidence of various cancers and birth defects.[^cite_note-\:3-408] Several studies have identified increased occurrence of deformities, cancers, and other serious health problems in areas where DU shells were used.[^cite_note-lancet2013-409] Some Iraqi doctors attributed these malformations to possible long-term effects of depleted uranium.[^cite_note-jaz1-410] Studies disagree on whether DU ammunition has any measurable detrimental health effects.[^cite_note-411][^cite_note-\:3-408] According to research from the [UK Atomic Energy Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_Atomic_Energy_Authority) in 2005, the cancer rate had increased by 35% since 2003. As of 2013, 140,000 Iraqis were suffering from cancer, with between 7,000 and 8,000 new cases yearly.[^cite_note-\:3-408] According to a 2012 journal article by Al-Hadithi et al., existing studies and research evidence does not show a "clear increase in birth defects" or a "clear indication of a possible environmental exposure including depleted uranium". The article further states that "there is actually no substantial evidence that genetic defects can arise from parental exposure to DU in any circumstances."[^cite_note-Al-Hadithi-412]

#### Ecosystem destruction

The war has also led to damage to ecosystems though pollution and physical destruction. Approximately 25,000 tons of bombs were dropped by the US military during the war.[^cite_note-\:1-404] More than 250 chemical and armament factories were destroyed, which caused over 50,000 cubic meters of [hazardous chemicals](1476975.md), such as fertilizer, and raw sewage to leak into water,[^cite_note-413] leading to surrounding freshwater ecosystem becoming polluted and species' habitat being impacted.[^cite_note-\:1-404] According to the [World Wide Fund for Nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Wide_Fund_for_Nature), 33 Iraqi wetlands, especially the Mesopotamian Marshland, have been contaminated by chemicals, which has caused 60 types of mammal species to lose their habitats.[^cite_note-\:2-406]

### Impact on the Global War on Terrorism

Main article: [Iraq War and the war on terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War_and_the_war_on_terror)

Further information: [Criticism of the war on terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_the_war_on_terrorism), [Saddam–al-Qaeda conspiracy theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam%E2%80%93al-Qaeda_conspiracy_theory), and [Timeline of Saddam–al-Qaeda conspiracy allegations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Saddam%E2%80%93al-Qaeda_conspiracy_allegations)

Though explicitly stating that Iraq had "nothing" to do with [9/11 attacks](5058690.md),[^cite_note-414] erstwhile President George W. Bush consistently referred to the Iraq War as "the central front in the [War on Terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_on_Terrorism)", and argued that if the United States pulled out of Iraq, "terrorists will follow us here".[^cite_note-415][^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417] While other proponents of the war regularly echoed this assertion, as the conflict dragged on, members of the US Congress, the US public, and even US troops questioned the connection between Iraq and the fight against anti-US terrorism. In particular, a consensus developed among intelligence experts that the Iraq War actually increased terrorism. [Counterterrorism](83392.md) expert [Rohan Gunaratna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohan_Gunaratna) frequently referred to the invasion of Iraq as a "fatal mistake".[^cite_note-418]

London's [International Institute for Strategic Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Strategic_Studies) concluded in 2004 that the occupation of Iraq had become "a potent global recruitment pretext" for [Mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen) and that the invasion "galvanised" al-Qaeda and "perversely inspired insurgent violence" there.[^cite_note-419] The US [National Intelligence Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Intelligence_Council_\(United_States\)) concluded in a January 2005 report that the war in Iraq had become a breeding ground for a new generation of terrorists; David Low, the national intelligence officer for transnational threats, indicated that the report concluded that the war in Iraq provided terrorists with "a training ground, a recruitment ground, the opportunity for enhancing technical skills ... There is even… the likelihood that some of the jihadists who are not killed there will… go home, wherever home is, and will, therefore, disperse to various other countries." The council's chairman [Robert Hutchings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Hutchings) said, "At the moment, Iraq is a magnet for international terrorist activity."[^cite_note-420] And the 2006 [National Intelligence Estimate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Intelligence_Estimate), which outlined the considered judgment of all 16 US intelligence agencies, held that "The Iraq conflict has become the 'cause célèbre' for jihadists, breeding a deep resentment of US involvement in the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world) and cultivating supporters for the global jihadist movement."[^cite_note-421]

A report by the [Council on Foreign Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_on_Foreign_Relations), released on the 20th anniversary of the invasion analyzed the rationale to go to war and the subsequent decisions during the occupation. The report states that the "justification for going to war was based on scanty and deeply flawed intelligence" and that the invasion was an "error compounded by the absence of an agreed exit strategy and the decision to embark on a massive, open-ended nation-building project". The same report also ascertained that "the occupation authority's first acts were to disband the Iraqi army and the Ba'athist governing party, igniting what would become a lethal, long-running insurgency and eventually a multinational terrorist organization that took over most of the country".[^cite_note-422][^cite_note-Schlosser_2023_6–25-106]

### Impact on geopolitics

Further information: [Ukrainian involvement in the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_involvement_in_the_Iraq_War), [Russo-Ukrainian War](42085878.md), and [United States and the Russian invasion of Ukraine](70376708.md)

From a geopolitical perspective, the war in Iraq has been interpreted as weakening the West's moral high ground and hampering its ability to effectively counter Russia and China. With regard to the [Russo-Ukrainian War](42085878.md), [Russian foreign minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_foreign_minister) [Sergey Lavrov](541346.md) said in March 2022 that the US exerted similar pressures on Iraq in 2003, which the US (and Ukraine) invaded later for what Lavrov described as no reason other than "a vial of unidentified chemicals".[^cite_note-423] In March 2023, [Tony Blair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Blair), former British prime minister rejected comparisons between Russia's war in Ukraine and the US-led invasion of Iraq, claiming that the Iraq War cannot be used as a justification by Russia to annex Russian-speaking zones in eastern Ukraine.[^cite_note-424]

## Criticism

Main article: [Criticism of the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_the_Iraq_War)

Further information: [Opposition to the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_to_the_Iraq_War), [Legitimacy of the 2003 invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legitimacy_of_the_2003_invasion_of_Iraq), [Legality of the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legality_of_the_Iraq_War), [Views on the 2003 invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Views_on_the_2003_invasion_of_Iraq), [Protests against the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protests_against_the_Iraq_War), [International reactions to the prelude to the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_reactions_to_the_prelude_to_the_Iraq_War), [United Nations Security Council and the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_and_the_Iraq_War), and [Media coverage of the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_coverage_of_the_Iraq_War)

See also: [Allegations of misappropriations related to the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegations_of_misappropriations_related_to_the_Iraq_War), [Habbush letter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habbush_letter), and [The Way of the World (book)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Way_of_the_World_\(book\))



A city street in [Ramadi](245655.md) heavily damaged by the fighting in 2006



A memorial in [North Carolina](21650.md) in December 2007; US casualty count can be seen in the background

[^cite_note-425]

The Bush administration's [rationale for the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationale_for_the_Iraq_War) has faced heavy criticism from an array of popular and official sources both inside and outside the United States,[^cite_note-426][^cite_note-427][^cite_note-pbs.org-361] with many US citizens finding many parallels with the [Vietnam War](32611.md).[^cite_note-428] For example, a former CIA officer described the [Office of Special Plans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Special_Plans) as a group of [ideologues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideologue) who were dangerous to US national security and a threat to world peace, and stated that the group lied and manipulated intelligence to further its agenda of removing Saddam.[^cite_note-429] The [Center for Public Integrity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Public_Integrity) stated that the [Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_W._Bush) made a total of 935 false statements between 2001 and 2003 about Iraq's alleged threat to the United States.[^cite_note-430]

Both proponents and [opponents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_to_the_Iraq_War) of the invasion have also criticized the prosecution of the war effort along with a number of other lines. Most significantly, critics have assailed the United States and its allies for not devoting enough troops to the mission, not adequately planning for [post-invasion Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Iraq_\(2003%E2%80%932011\)), and for permitting and perpetrating human rights abuses. As the war has progressed, critics have also railed against the high human and financial costs. In 2016, the United Kingdom published the [Iraq Inquiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Inquiry), a public inquiry which was broadly critical of the actions of the British government and military in making the case for the war, in tactics and in planning for the aftermath of the war.[^cite_note-GuardianReport-431][^cite_note-TelegraphReport-432][^cite_note-BBCReport-433]

- Human rights violations such as the [Iraq prison abuse scandals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_prison_abuse_scandals)

- Insufficient post-invasion plans, in particular inadequate troop levels (A [RAND Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAND_Corporation) study stated that 500,000 troops would be required for success.)

- [Financial costs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_cost_of_the_2003_Iraq_Conflict) with approximately \$612 billion spent as of 4/09 the [CBO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Budget_Office) has estimated the total cost of the war in Iraq to the United States will be around \$1.9 trillion.

## Human rights abuses

Throughout the war, many human rights abuses and war crimes were committed.

### By coalition forces and private contractors

See also: [United States war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_war_crimes) and [British war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_war_crimes)



This photograph from [Abu Ghraib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ghraib_torture_and_prisoner_abuse) prison released in 2006 shows a pyramid of abused Iraqi prisoners

- Deaths of civilians as a result of bombing and missile strikes that fail to take feasible precautions with regards to civilians casualties.

  [^cite_note-455]

- [Abu Ghraib torture and prisoner abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ghraib_torture_and_prisoner_abuse) by US Army personnel,

  [^cite_note-456]

  &#x20;involving the detention of thousands of Iraqi people. Torture at Abu Ghraib included rape, sodomy and extensive sexual abuse, waterboarding, pouring [phosphoric acid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phosphoric_acid) on detainees, sleep deprivation and physical beatings.

- [Haditha massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haditha_massacre) of 24 civilians by US soldiers.

- Widespread use of the incendiary munition [white phosphorus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_phosphorus_use_in_Iraq) such as during the battle of Fallujah. The documentary *[Fallujah, The Hidden Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fallujah,_The_Hidden_Massacre)*, claimed that Iraqi civilians, including women and children, had died of burns caused by white phosphorus during the battle, however, US Department of Defense spokesman Lieutenant Colonel Barry Venable denied that this was true but confirmed to the BBC that US forces had used white phosphorus as an incendiary weapon there against enemy combatants.

  [^cite_note-457]

  [^cite_note-458]

  [^cite_note-459]

  &#x20;The use of white phosphorus against civilian populations is banned by international legislation.

  [^cite_note-460]

- [Mahmudiyah rape and killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmudiyah_rape_and_killings), where US soldiers raped and killed 14-year old Abeer Qasim Humza. They also killed 3 of her relatives.

  [^cite_note-461]

  [^cite_note-462]

- The torture and killing of [prisoner of war](25008.md), Iraqi Air Force commander, [Abed Hamed Mowhoush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abed_Hamed_Mowhoush).

- The [killing of Baha Mousa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Baha_Mousa) while in British Army custody.

- [Mukaradeeb wedding party massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukaradeeb_wedding_party_massacre), where 42 civilians were allegedly killed by coalition airstrikes.

  [^cite_note-463]

- Planting weapons on noncombatant, unarmed Iraqis by three US Marines after killing them.

  [^cite_note-464]

  [^cite_note-465]

  &#x20;According to a report by *[The Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Nation)*, other similar acts have been witnessed by US soldiers.

  [^cite_note-466]

- [Nisour Square massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nisour_Square_massacre) by Blackwater Security Consulting personnel.

- Allegations of beatings, [electrocution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrocution), [mock executions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mock_execution), and [sexual assault](218407.md) by British troops were presented to the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) (ICC) by Public Interest Lawyers (PIL) and the [European Center for Constitutional and Human Rights](31683151.md) (ECCHR) on 12 January 2014.

  [^cite_note-467]

- [United Nations reports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Assistance_Mission_for_Iraq) and human rights organizations documented allegations of sexual violence against Iraqi women during [coalition forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-National_Force_%E2%80%93_Iraq) night raids and detention operations, including forced nudity, molestation, physical assaults, and sexual threats.

  [^cite_note-UNAMI_2008-468]

  [^cite_note-UNAMI_2007-469]

  [^cite_note-Amnesty2004-470]

  [^cite_note-MADRE2007-471]

  &#x20;Iraqi women held in US forces custody, including those arrested on suspicion of insurgency or as relatives of suspects, were reported to have been subjected to rape, sexualized torture, and threats of sexual violence to extract information, induce male relatives to surrender, or as a form of collective punishment.

  [^cite_note-UNAMI_2008-468]

  [^cite_note-UNAMI_2007-469]

  [^cite_note-Amnesty2004-470]

  [^cite_note-MADRE2007-471]

  [^cite_note-Amnesty_2005-472]

### By insurgent groups

Main article: [List of bombings during the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_bombings_during_the_Iraq_War)

Further information: [List of bombings during the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_bombings_during_the_Iraq_War) and [Tactics of the Iraqi insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tactics_of_the_Iraqi_insurgency)



Car bombing was a frequently used tactic by insurgents in Iraq

- Killing over 12,000 Iraqis from January 2005 to June 2006, according to Iraqi Interior Minister [Bayan Jabr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayan_Jabr), giving the first official count for the victims of bombings, ambushes and other deadly attacks.

  [^cite_note-473]

  &#x20;The insurgents have also conducted numerous [suicide attacks](18835454.md) on the Iraqi civilian population, mostly targeting the majority Shia community.

  [^cite_note-474]

  [^cite_note-475]

  &#x20;An October 2005 report from [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) examines the range of civilian attacks and their purported justification.

  [^cite_note-476]

- Attacks against civilians by sectarian death squads primarily during the [Iraqi Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_war_in_Iraq_\(2006-07\)). Iraq Body Count project data shows that 33% of civilian deaths during the Iraq War resulted from execution after abduction or capture. These were overwhelmingly carried out by unknown actors including insurgents, sectarian militias and criminals.

  [^cite_note-477]

- Attacks on diplomats and diplomatic facilities including; the bombing of the UN headquarters in Baghdad in August 2003 killing the top UN representative in Iraq and 21 other UN staff members;

  [^cite_note-478]

  &#x20;beheading several diplomats: two Algerian diplomatic envoys Ali Belaroussi and Azzedine Belkadi,

  [^cite_note-479]

  &#x20;Egyptian diplomatic envoy al-Sherif,

  [^cite_note-480]

  &#x20;and four Russian diplomats

  [^cite_note-481]

- The February 2006 bombing of the [al-Askari Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Askari_Mosque) severely damaged one of the holiest Shiite shrines. [Sectarian strife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sectarian_strife) and reprisal killings followed.

  [^cite_note-482]

- The publicised killing of several contractors; [Eugene Armstrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugene_Armstrong), [Jack Hensley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Hensley), [Kenneth Bigley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenneth_Bigley), Ivaylo Kepov and Georgi Lazov (Bulgarian truck drivers.)

  [^cite_note-483]

  &#x20;Other non-military personnel murdered include: translator [Kim Sun-il](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Sun-il), [Shosei Koda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shosei_Koda), [Fabrizio Quattrocchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fabrizio_Quattrocchi) (Italian), charity worker [Margaret Hassan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Hassan), reconstruction engineer [Nick Berg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Berg), photographer Salvatore Santoro (Italian)

  [^cite_note-484]

  &#x20;and supply worker [Seif Adnan Kanaan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seif_Adnan_Kanaan) (Iraqi.) Four private armed contractors, Scott Helvenston, Jerko Zovko, Wesley Batalona and Michael Teague, were killed with grenades and small arms fire, their bodies dragged from their vehicles, beaten and set ablaze. Their burned corpses were then dragged through the streets before being hung over a bridge crossing the Euphrates.

  [^cite_note-485]

- Torture or killing of members of the [New Iraqi Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Iraqi_Army),

  [^cite_note-486]

  &#x20;and assassination of civilians associated with the [Coalition Provisional Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Provisional_Authority), such as [Fern Holland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fern_Holland), or the [Iraqi Governing Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Governing_Council), such as [Aqila al-Hashimi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqila_al-Hashimi) and [Ezzedine Salim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ezzedine_Salim), or other foreign civilians, such as those from Kenya

  [^cite_note-487]

### By post-invasion Iraqi Government

Main article: [Human rights in post-invasion Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_post-invasion_Iraq)

The post-invasion Iraqi government used torture against detainees, including children. Some techniques of torture used included beatings, electric shocks, prolonged hanging by the wrists, food and water deprivation, and blindfolding for multiple days.[^cite_note-488] Iraqi police from the Interior Ministry were accused of forming [death squads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_squad) and committing numerous massacres of Sunni Arabs.[^cite_note-489] Many of these human rights abuses were carried out by Iraqi government-sponsored Shi'ite militias.[^cite_note-490]

According to studies, most of the suicide bombers in [Iraq](7515928.md) were foreigners, especially [Saudis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_people).[^cite_note-suicide-bombers-in-iraq-491][^cite_note-492][^cite_note-493]

### Role of Iran

Main article: [Iranian involvement in the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_involvement_in_the_Iraq_War)

According to two unnamed US officials, [the Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pentagon) is examining the possibility that the [Karbala provincial headquarters raid](9129813.md), in which insurgents managed to infiltrate an American base, kill five US soldiers, wound three, and destroy three humvees before fleeing, was supported by Iranians. In a speech on 31 January 2007, Iraqi Prime Minister [Nouri al-Maliki](4723094.md) stated that Iran was supporting attacks against coalition forces in Iraq[^cite_note-494] and some Iraqis suspect that the raid may have been perpetrated by the Quds Force in retaliation for the detention of five Iranian officials by US forces in the northern Iraqi city of [Irbil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irbil) on 11 January.[^cite_note-495][^cite_note-496] In 2014, the legacy of Iran's presence in Iraq after the invasion had been mixed with regard to the fight against regional terrorist groups. The US occupation and subsequent regional instability had spawned the creation of the PMF ([Popular Mobilization Forces](45671374.md)), an Iranian militia that effectively fought the influence of emerging caliphates in the region.[^cite_note-497]

Later, a 1,300-page US Army Iraq War study, released in January 2019, concluded that "At the time of this project's completion in 2018, an emboldened and expansionist Iran appears to be the only victor" and that the outcome of the war triggered a "deep skepticism about foreign interventions" among America's public opinion.[^cite_note-us-army-iran-victor-438]

[Hezbollah](13919.md) as Iran's proxy, formed [Unit 3800](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unit_3800?action=edit\&redlink=1) and sent elite Hezbollah operatives to Iraq to train local fighters. The unit's primary mission was to train and equip Iraqi Shiite militias, such as the [Mahdi Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahdi_Army), [Asaib Ahl al-Haq](26106154.md), and [Kataib Hezbollah](23626646.md), enhancing their capabilities in guerrilla warfare, kidnappings, and the use of sophisticated improvised explosive devices (IEDs). Some Iraqi militants also received advanced training in [Lebanon](17771.md). The unit then oversaw operations executed against US and coalition forces and provided funds, weapons, and logistical assistance to groups like the [Badr Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badr_Organization), [Saraya al-Khorasani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saraya_al-Khorasani), and the al-Mahdi Army.[^cite_note-498][^cite_note-499]

### Role of Israel

This section is an excerpt from [International reactions to the prelude to the Iraq War § Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_reactions_to_the_prelude_to_the_Iraq_War#Israel).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=International_reactions_to_the_prelude_to_the_Iraq_War\&action=edit)]

Further information: [Iraq-Israel relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq-Israel_relations)

Israel did not support or take part in the Iraq War. According to former [State Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Department_of_State) official [Lawrence Wilkerson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Wilkerson) and former [Central Intelligence Agency](5183633.md) official and Iran expert [Robert Baer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Baer), Israeli officials warned the Bush administration against invading Iraq, saying that it would destabilize the region and empower the much more dangerous regime in Iran.[^cite_note-500][^cite_note-501][^cite_note-502][^cite_note-503] According to former U.S. [Under Secretary of Defense for Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Under_Secretary_of_Defense_for_Policy) [Douglas Feith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Feith), Israeli officials did not push their American counterparts to initiate the war in Iraq. In an interview with [Ynet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ynet), Feith stated that "what you heard from the Israelis was not any kind of advocacy of war with Iraq" and that "\[w]hat you heard from Israeli officials in private discussions was that they were not really focused on Iraq... \[t]hey were much more focused on Iran."[^cite_note-504]

Nonetheless, Israeli officials expressed concerns about Saddam Hussein. In August 2002, *[Haaretz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haaretz)* reported that Israeli intelligence provided Washington with reports about Iraq's alleged program to develop weapons of mass destruction.[^cite_note-505] In the same month, the *[Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Post)* reported that "Israel is urging United States' officials not to delay a military strike against Iraq's Saddam Hussein".[^cite_note-506] In September 2002, [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md), testifying under oath as a private citizen before the [U.S. House of Representatives Government Reform Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Committee_on_Oversight_and_Government_Reform), lobbied for the [invasion of Iraq](201936.md) and said: "There is no question whatsoever that [Saddam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) is seeking and is working and is advancing towards the development of nuclear weapons…"[^cite_note-507][^cite_note-508][^cite_note-International_reactions_to_the_prelude_to_the_Iraq_War_\:1-509] He also said, "If you take out Saddam, Saddam's regime, I guarantee you that it will have enormous positive reverberations on the region."[^cite_note-International_reactions_to_the_prelude_to_the_Iraq_War_\:1-509]

In January 2007, *[The Forward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Forward)* reported that, before March 2003, Israeli Prime Minister [Ariel Sharon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariel_Sharon) told Bush that Israel "would not push one way or the other" for or against an Iraq war.[^cite_note-510] According to this report, Sharon said that he believed that Iraq was a genuine threat to the Middle East and had weapons of mass destruction. However, he warned Bush that, if the US did go to war with Iraq, he should make sure to formulate a viable exit strategy, prepare a counterinsurgency strategy, and should not attempt to implant democracy in the Arab world.

After the invasion, Israel shared its expertise on [counterinsurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterinsurgency) methods, such as utilizing drones and operating checkpoints.[^cite_note-511]

### Role of Russia

The [invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Iraq) prompted a widespread wave of criticism from several world leaders, including Russian President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md).[^cite_note-512] Before and during the invasion of Iraq, the Russian government provided intelligence to Saddam Hussein about the location of US forces and their plans.[^cite_note-advances-513]

## Public opinion on the war

Main article: [Public opinion in the United States on the invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_opinion_in_the_United_States_on_the_invasion_of_Iraq)

Americans polled in January 2003 widely favored diplomacy over an invasion. Later that year, however, Americans began to agree with Bush's plan. The US government engaged in an elaborate domestic public relations campaign to promote the war to its citizens. Americans overwhelmingly believed Saddam did have weapons of mass destruction: 85% said so, even though the inspectors had not uncovered those weapons. By February 2003, 64% of Americans supported taking military action to remove Saddam from power.[^cite_note-CBSnews-20030124-514] In a March 2003 [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_\(company\)) poll, the day after the invasion, 76% of Americans had approved of military action against Iraq.[^cite_note-515]

### International opinion



Protesters on 19 March 2005, in [London](17867.md), where over 150,000 marched

In a March 2003 [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) poll, 54% of Britons supported the military action against Iraq.[^cite_note-516] A remarkable aspect was the support for invasion expressed by many left-wing intellectuals such as [Christopher Hitchens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Hitchens), [Paul Berman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Berman), [Michael Walzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Walzer) and [Jean Bethke Elshtain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Bethke_Elshtain).[^cite_note-517][^cite_note-518] In a February 2003 poll by the national public research institute [CIS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_de_Investigaciones_Sociol%C3%B3gicas), 91% of Spaniards opposed any military intervention in Iraq.[^cite_note-519]

According to a January 2007 [BBC World Service](37627.md) poll of more than 26,000 people in 25 countries, 73% of the global population disapproved of US handling of the Iraq War.[^cite_note-520] A September 2007 poll conducted by the BBC found that two-thirds of the world's population believed the US should withdraw its forces from Iraq.[^cite_note-521]

In 2006 it was found that majorities in the UK and Canada believed that the war in Iraq was "unjustified" and – in the UK – were critical of their government's support of US policies in Iraq.[^cite_note-522]

According to polls conducted by the [Arab American Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_American_Institute), four years after the invasion of Iraq, 83% of Egyptians had a negative view of the US role in Iraq; 68% of Saudi Arabians had a negative view; 96% of the Jordanian population had a negative view; 70% of the population of the United Arab Emirates and 76% of the Lebanese population also described their view as negative.[^cite_note-523] The Pew Global Attitudes Project reports that in 2006 majorities in the [Netherlands](21148.md), Germany, [Jordan](7515964.md), France, [Lebanon](17771.md), Russia, China, Canada, [Poland](22936.md), [Pakistan](23235.md), Spain, [Indonesia](14579.md), [Turkey](11125639.md), and [Morocco](19291.md) believed the world was safer before the Iraq War and the toppling of Saddam, while pluralities in the United States and [India](14533.md) believe the world is safer without Saddam Hussein.[^cite_note-524]

#### Iraqi opinion



A woman pleads with an [Iraqi army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_army) soldier from 2nd Company, 5th Brigade, 2nd Iraqi Army Division to let a suspected insurgent free during a raid near Tafaria, [Iraq](7515928.md)

Directly after the invasion, a [NDTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NDTV) and a [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup,_Inc.) polls of Baghdad residents reported that a slight majority of respondents supported the US invasion.[^cite_note-525] Polls conducted between 2005 and 2007 showed 31–37% of Iraqis wanted coalition forces to withdraw once security was restored and that 26–35% wanted immediate withdrawal.[^cite_note-WorldPublicOpinion-526][^cite_note-bbcpoll-527][^cite_note-528] In 2006, a poll conducted on the Iraqi public revealed that 52% of the ones polled said Iraq was going in the right direction and 61% claimed it was worth ousting Saddam Hussein.[^cite_note-WorldPublicOpinion-526] In a March 2007 [BBC](19344654.md) poll, 82% of Iraqis expressed a lack of confidence in coalition forces.[^cite_note-529] According to a 2009 poll conducted by the University of Maryland, 7 out of 10 Iraqis wanted US troops to withdraw within one year and 78% felt that US military presence was "provoking more conflict than it is preventing".[^cite_note-530] Despite a majority having previously been opposed to the US presence, according to a poll conducted by the Asharq Research Centre, a private Iraqi company, 60% of Iraqis had believed it was "the wrong time" for a major withdrawal of American troops prior to the withdrawal in 2011, with 51% saying withdrawal would have a negative effect.[^cite_note-531][^cite_note-532]
